# OpenAI SDR Final Sales Acumen Interview Pack

## Purpose

This is the question-by-question drafting surface for the Phase 3A Sales Acumen interview. It does not replace [[OpenAI-SDR-sales-acumen-interview-pack]] yet. It becomes canonical only after every core answer is drafted, rehearsed, reconciled and audited.

The structure is intentionally exhaustive. It covers the complete deal lifecycle, the seller operating system around the deal, performance under pressure, Account Director partnership and two symmetrical flagship-case drills.

## Final-Round Priority Answer Modules

### FR-S1. How do you use AI today, and how would you use it at OpenAI?

> [!tip] FINAL-ROUND EDIT 2026-07-26: NEW + MERGED
> This is the shared answer family for July 22 questions 3, 5, and 6. Lead with the range of use cases. Go deeper only if the interviewer asks for one implementation.

> [!question] FINAL-ROUND EDIT 2026-07-26: WORKFLOW SELECTION WORKSHEET
> The table below is the repo-derived menu for composing this answer. It is deliberately broader than anything Seth should say in one response. Select one research workflow, one judgment/sparring workflow, one proof-building workflow, and optionally one RevOps or operating-system workflow. The spoken answer below remains provisional until those choices are made.

#### Strategic BDR AI Workflow Selection Worksheet

> [!important] FINAL-ROUND EDIT 2026-07-26: READ THE TABLE ON TWO AXES
> This is comprehensive for the practical ways a Strategic BDR could use OpenAI's current capability stack; it is not a literal inventory of every product feature and it is much broader than one spoken answer. Read each row as **business workflow × best modality or surface**. The modality column answers what Seth would actually use: Chat, GPT-Live Voice, audio analysis, Deep Research/web search, files and data, vision, image or Sora video generation, browser/computer interaction, ChatGPT Work, Codex, workspace agents, apps/connectors, scheduled tasks, or a custom API workflow. Availability and permissions can vary by plan, rollout, workspace settings, and connected source.
>
> **Voice does not mean dictation here.** ChatGPT Voice, powered by GPT-Live, is a live, interruptible working conversation. Seth can talk through an account, rehearse discovery or objections, start or steer work in Chat, Work, or Codex, check progress, and—when screen context is enabled on macOS—ask ChatGPT to inspect the frontmost window. Dictation is only the narrower speech-to-text option.

**How to use it:** mark the workflows that feel most authentic, then build the live answer around three or four complementary examples. Do not read the table as a script, list every modality aloud, or imply that a proposed OpenAI-role workflow is something Seth has already run.

##### OpenAI modality and surface map for this answer

| Modality or surface                            | What Seth would use it for as a Strategic BDR                                                                                                                                                                             |
| ---------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Chat / text reasoning**                      | Quick questions, account-thesis sparring, discovery design, concise drafting, comparison, and judgment support.                                                                                                           |
| **ChatGPT Voice powered by GPT-Live**          | Natural day-to-day conversation, interruptible brainstorming, live role-play, objection practice, verbal meeting prep, and starting/checking/steering work across Chat, Work, and Codex. This is distinct from dictation. |
| **Audio inputs and transcription**             | Review permitted call recordings, voice notes, and audio files; extract buyer language, decisions, objections, questions, and follow-up items. This is asynchronous analysis, distinct from a live Voice conversation.    |
| **Deep Research and web search**               | Multi-source company, market, stakeholder, industry, and competitive research with traceable sources; use Deep Research when the task requires planning and synthesis rather than a quick lookup.                         |
| **Files, documents, and data analysis**        | Inspect PDFs, transcripts, documents, CSVs, spreadsheets, presentations, and dashboard exports; calculate, compare, summarize, and create charts or tables.                                                               |
| **Vision and screen context**                  | Inspect screenshots, product interfaces, diagrams, dashboards, visual workflows, and—when enabled—an appshot of the current macOS window.                                                                                 |
| **Image generation and editing**               | Create or edit buyer-specific visuals, mockups, concept images, simple explainers, deck assets, and demo-supporting imagery; review dense text and production-critical assets carefully.                                  |
| **Video generation with Sora**                 | Create or edit short visual explainers, concept clips, event assets, or demo-supporting media from text or image references. This is optional breadth for the role, not a required lead example.                          |
| **Browser and computer interaction**           | Navigate approved sites or apps, verify live workflows, capture evidence, test an experience, and perform bounded interactions with visible review and permission controls.                                               |
| **ChatGPT Work**                               | Delegate longer, multi-step work that should end in a review-ready brief, deck, spreadsheet, analysis, recurring update, workflow, or file.                                                                               |
| **Codex coding agents**                        | Build and test lightweight demos, calculators, browser workflows, internal tools, data-cleaning scripts, interactive visualizations, and repeatable skills; review code and verify the result.                            |
| **Codex multi-agent workflows**                | Split independent research, exploration, test, review, or document-analysis tasks across parallel agents and bring distilled results back to the main task.                                                               |
| **Workspace agents, apps, and connectors**     | Run shared, repeatable workflows across approved calendars, email, CRM, knowledge stores, Slack, SharePoint, Drive, or other connected systems with scoped permissions.                                                   |
| **Scheduled tasks and automations**            | Monitor, refresh, triage, or prepare recurring work such as morning briefs, meeting prep, signal checks, research digests, and open-loop sweeps.                                                                          |
| **API, Responses, Realtime, and tool calling** | Build a custom or embedded workflow when Chat, Work, Codex, or a workspace agent is not the right surface—for example a bespoke account tool, voice experience, structured data pipeline, or system-triggered agent.      |

**Current official modality basis checked 2026-07-26:** [ChatGPT Voice](https://learn.chatgpt.com/docs/features/voice), [ChatGPT Work](https://learn.chatgpt.com/docs/get-started-with-work), [image inputs](https://learn.chatgpt.com/docs/image-inputs), [image generation](https://learn.chatgpt.com/docs/image-generation), [Sora video generation](https://developers.openai.com/api/docs/guides/video-generation), [tabular-data analysis](https://learn.chatgpt.com/use-cases/analyze-data-export), [Codex subagents](https://learn.chatgpt.com/docs/agent-configuration/subagents), and [workspace-agent sales meeting prep](https://developers.openai.com/cookbook/articles/chatgpt-agents-sales-meeting-prep). Product availability and behavior should be rechecked before a future interview if the pack is reused.

##### What else Seth could do specifically with Codex

Beyond “write code,” Codex can support the sales motion by:

- building and testing a buyer-specific prototype, ROI tool, workflow simulator, or interactive demo;
- turning successful research, meeting-prep, handoff, or CRM processes into reusable skills and repeatable agent instructions;
- cleaning, joining, deduplicating, enriching, and quality-checking CRM or territory exports with inspectable scripts;
- automating browser-based evidence gathering or live-journey verification while preserving source URLs and uncertainty;
- creating interactive HTML dashboards, account maps, calculators, and visualizations from sales data;
- building evals and regression checks so a classification, extraction, research, or drafting workflow can be tested on known examples;
- using parallel agents for independent account research, source verification, technical review, or comparison work;
- connecting repositories, files, browsers, approved apps, MCP tools, and APIs into one reviewable workflow; and
- testing, debugging, reviewing, and documenting the artifact before it is used with a buyer.

The boundary remains the same: Codex can build, inspect, test, and draft; Seth owns the commercial judgment, customer truth, relationship, and external commitment.

**Answer-role labels:**

- **Lead** — strong candidate for the first 60 seconds.
- **Proof** — concrete example to make the answer credible.
- **Breadth** — useful to show range after the core examples.
- **Depth** — keep for follow-up rather than the opening.

##### 1. Research, account intelligence, and prioritization

| Pick | Workflow | What AI contributes | Human-owned boundary | Modality / OpenAI surface | Answer role |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| ☐ | Source-backed Second Brain and QMD retrieval | Searches exact terms and semantic matches across notes, transcripts, research, and maintained wiki pages before producing a synthesis. | Seth checks the original source, freshness, permissions, and whether the retrieved material actually answers the question. | **Codex + files/local retrieval + Chat** | Lead |
| ☐ | One-page account brief | Combines approved internal context and external research into the account thesis, current initiatives, likely pains, open questions, relevant proof, and suggested next action. | The seller verifies material facts and decides what matters commercially. | **ChatGPT Work + Deep Research + approved apps/connectors** | Lead |
| ☐ | Territory and TAM mapping | Builds a structured universe of accounts from multiple sources, normalizes the data, and helps reveal segments the CRM may miss. | The team defines the territory, ICP, exclusions, and acceptable evidence. | **Deep Research + Codex + file/data analysis** | Proof |
| ☐ | ICP and anti-ICP classification | Compares companies with a defined rubric or golden set and identifies likely fit, non-fit, and uncertain cases. | Sellers own the rubric, inspect samples, and decide ambiguous or strategic accounts. | **File/data analysis + Codex classification workflow** | Proof |
| ☐ | Account enrichment and entity resolution | Finds missing firmographic, contact, service, location, or technology evidence; proposes duplicate, branch, and parent relationships. | Inferences remain confidence-labeled; uncertain matches do not become CRM fact without review. | **Deep Research/web search + Codex + file/data analysis** | Proof |
| ☐ | Signal monitoring and messageability | Watches approved first-, second-, and third-party signals, then separates “worth monitoring” from “credible reason to contact now.” | The seller decides whether the signal is current, relevant, respectful, and strong enough to spend the account's attention. | **Workspace agent + apps/connectors + web search + schedule** | Lead |
| ☐ | Stakeholder and buying-group mapping | Suggests workflow owners, champions, technical validators, security, procurement, finance, and executive stakeholders from available evidence. | People and influence cannot be treated as verified merely because a model inferred them; the seller validates the map through discovery. | **Deep Research + company knowledge/apps + Chat** | Lead |
| ☐ | Warm-path and relationship research | Looks for shared context, introductions, prior conversations, role changes, communities, events, and credible paths into the account. | The human judges relationship strength, social appropriateness, and whether an introduction should be requested. | **Deep Research + approved CRM/email/calendar/connectors** | Breadth |
| ☐ | Current-stack and competitive-context research | Collects evidence about the buyer's existing tools, public AI initiatives, developer estate, and likely integration constraints. | Public clues are hypotheses, not proof of internal usage, satisfaction, contract status, or replacement intent. | **Deep Research/web search + Codex/browser verification** | Breadth |
| ☐ | Similar-account and proof-point matching | Retrieves the most relevant approved case, workflow, objection response, or adjacent proof for the account's situation. | The seller checks comparability and never implies that another customer's outcome will automatically transfer. | **Company knowledge/files + retrieval + Chat** | Lead |
| ☐ | Account prioritization and “top three today” queue | Ranks accounts using fit, signal strength, urgency, access, messageability, and proof proximity; explains why each deserves attention. | The seller owns final priority, territory judgment, capacity allocation, and any exception for a strategic account. | **File/data analysis + ChatGPT Work + approved CRM connector** | Lead |
| ☐ | Research freshness and contradiction check | Flags stale fields, conflicting sources, missing evidence, and facts that should be re-queried live before use. | Seth resolves contradictions or leaves the field unknown; the model does not choose the convenient version. | **Deep Research/web search + Codex + source-linked files** | Depth |

##### 2. Sparring partner and seller judgment

| Pick | Workflow | What AI contributes | Human-owned boundary | Modality / OpenAI surface | Answer role |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| ☐ | Account-thesis pressure test | Attacks the “why this account, why now, why this buyer, why this workflow” logic and names weak assumptions or missing evidence. | The seller chooses the thesis and remains accountable for pursuing or dropping it. | **ChatGPT Voice for live sparring + Chat for written critique** | Lead |
| ☐ | Workflow hypothesis generation | Generates several possible high-value workflows from the account context and maps each to owner, value, urgency, feasibility, and proof step. | Discovery, not model enthusiasm, determines which workflow is real. | **ChatGPT Voice + Chat + attached account files** | Lead |
| ☐ | Use-case prioritization | Compares hypotheses by business value, urgency, access, data readiness, governance burden, and proof proximity. | The AD and seller make the commercial call; scoring is decision support, not an automatic qualification decision. | **Chat + file/data analysis + ChatGPT Work** | Breadth |
| ☐ | Discovery-question design | Converts the account thesis into questions about the current process, root cause, baseline, prior attempts, decision process, risk, and success criteria. | The seller listens, follows the conversation, and does not mechanically run a generated checklist. | **ChatGPT Voice + Chat** | Lead |
| ☐ | Persona and stakeholder simulation | Rehearses how an operator, executive, security leader, technical evaluator, finance buyer, or procurement stakeholder may react. | Simulations are preparation, not evidence of what a real person believes. | **ChatGPT Voice powered by GPT-Live** | Breadth |
| ☐ | Objection rehearsal and role-play | Plays a skeptical prospect, challenges the value case, and helps Seth practice concise responses and follow-up questions. | Seth selects language that is truthful and natural; the model cannot authorize claims or commitments. | **ChatGPT Voice powered by GPT-Live** | Lead |
| ☐ | Messageability test | Reviews whether an outreach angle is specific, timely, non-obvious, buyer-relevant, and supported by evidence rather than generic personalization. | The seller decides whether to contact, wait, use a warm path, or suppress the message. | **Chat + account files/company knowledge** | Lead |
| ☐ | Qualification and disqualification sparring | Tests whether there is a real problem, owner, reason now, path to power, viable OpenAI fit, and credible next step. | Humans own qualification, disqualification, and the consequences of CRM stage changes. | **ChatGPT Voice + Chat + CRM/account context** | Breadth |
| ☐ | Deal-risk and next-step inspection | Surfaces missing stakeholders, weak champions, generic next steps, absent proof, procurement risk, or an internal story the buyer cannot yet repeat. | The seller and AD validate the risk and decide the intervention. | **ChatGPT Work + apps/connectors + Chat** | Depth |
| ☐ | Call or answer coaching | Reviews a transcript or draft answer for clarity, excessive technical depth, unsupported claims, missed questions, and stronger opening language. | Seth owns his voice and decides which feedback is correct. | **ChatGPT Voice + audio/transcript/file analysis** | Lead |

##### 3. Making value tangible

| Pick | Workflow | What AI contributes | Human-owned boundary | Modality / OpenAI surface | Answer role |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| ☐ | Buyer-specific workflow diagram | Turns discovery into a simple current-state and future-state flow showing actors, systems, decisions, exceptions, and review points. | The buyer validates the workflow; the diagram is a hypothesis, not a production architecture promise. | **Codex or ChatGPT Work + vision/diagram artifact** | Lead |
| ☐ | Lightweight demo or prototype | Creates a narrow proof artifact using safe or synthetic inputs so the buyer can react to something concrete. | Seth frames it as proof of preparation; technical specialists validate feasibility, security, and production design. | **Codex coding agent + image generation + vision for verification** | Lead |
| ☐ | ROI or business-case calculator | Structures baseline volume, time, cost, errors, conversion, or capacity assumptions and makes sensitivity visible. | The buyer owns the inputs; unverified assumptions stay labeled and no forecast is presented as guaranteed value. | **Codex + spreadsheet/data analysis + ChatGPT Work** | Proof |
| ☐ | Use-case-to-product mapping | Maps a validated workflow to the most plausible OpenAI surface and the questions that determine fit. | Seth does not force every use case into one product or make current product claims without checking approved material. | **Chat + approved product files/company knowledge** | Breadth |
| ☐ | Buyer-defined demo brief and success criteria | Converts the buyer's “what would this need to prove?” into a focused demo, evaluation, or PoC scorecard. | The buyer and account team agree the decision criteria; AI cannot manufacture sponsor commitment. | **ChatGPT Voice for co-thinking + ChatGPT Work for the scorecard** | Lead |
| ☐ | Champion memo or executive one-pager | Drafts a forwardable account-specific story: problem, why now, proposed workflow, value, risk controls, and requested next step. | The champion and seller verify the language, claims, and internal political fit. | **ChatGPT Work + account files + approved apps/connectors** | Lead |
| ☐ | Personalized deck or account artifact | Assembles sourced slides, workflow visuals, relevant proof, and open questions for a specific buyer conversation. | The seller controls narrative, evidence, brand, and every customer-facing claim. | **ChatGPT Work + image generation + files/presentations** | Proof |
| ☐ | Short visual explainer or concept video | Turns a validated account or workflow idea into a concise visual narrative, animated concept, or demo-supporting clip. | Seth verifies every claim, brand element, likeness, permission, and implication; generated media is an illustration, not proof that the workflow exists in production. | **Sora video generation + image generation + ChatGPT Work** | Depth |
| ☐ | Evaluation or PoC plan | Drafts scope, data path, owners, success measures, approval gates, timeline, risks, and the scale-or-stop decision. | Account, technical, security, and customer stakeholders approve the actual plan. | **ChatGPT Work + Codex + files/data analysis** | Depth |
| ☐ | Proposal or SOW first draft | Retrieves approved context and templates, then prepares a review-ready commercial or implementation draft. | Pricing, scope, legal language, acceptance criteria, and commitments require explicit human ownership. | **ChatGPT Work + document files + approved company knowledge/apps** | Proof / Depth |

##### 4. Outreach, meetings, and follow-through

| Pick | Workflow | What AI contributes | Human-owned boundary | Modality / OpenAI surface | Answer role |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| ☐ | Evidence-backed outreach first draft | Turns an approved account thesis and signal into concise outreach tailored to the stakeholder and likely workflow. | The seller checks truth, tone, relevance, channel, and send decision. | **Chat + company knowledge/account files** | Lead |
| ☐ | Persona and channel variants | Produces alternative versions for executive, operator, technical, or partner audiences and for email, LinkedIn, or event follow-up. | Seth selects the right audience and does not automate false familiarity. | **Chat + ChatGPT Voice for tone rehearsal** | Breadth |
| ☐ | Cadence, cooldown, and re-engagement suggestions | Recommends whether to contact now, monitor, nurture, change channel, or wait for a stronger reason. | The seller owns persistence, account fatigue, suppression, and relationship judgment. | **Workspace agent + CRM/email apps + scheduled task** | Breadth |
| ☐ | Meeting prebrief | Pulls account history, attendee context, open loops, likely objections, relevant proof, and the desired meeting outcome into a short brief. | Seth verifies the facts and chooses the meeting strategy. | **Workspace agent + calendar/CRM/knowledge apps + Deep Research** | Lead |
| ☐ | Call capture and structured summary | Converts approved notes or a transcript into decisions, buyer language, objections, proof gaps, commitments, and unresolved questions. | The seller checks accuracy, consent, sensitivity, and whether the summary reflects the actual conversation. | **Audio/transcript/file analysis + ChatGPT Work** | Lead |
| ☐ | Follow-up and next-step draft | Drafts a concise recap with value discussed, decisions, owners, dates, unanswered questions, and the next meeting. | Seth approves the message and every commitment before sending. | **Chat or ChatGPT Work + email/CRM apps** | Lead |
| ☐ | Post-meeting pulse | Combines the meeting with account history to update the thesis, stakeholder map, risks, evidence gaps, and next actions. | Important changes remain reviewable and source-linked rather than silently becoming truth. | **ChatGPT Work + transcript/files + CRM/company knowledge** | Proof |
| ☐ | AD handoff packet | Produces a compact account thesis, stakeholder map, buyer language, qualification evidence, proof gaps, risks, and recommended next step. | The BDR owns handoff quality; the AD reviews and decides how to advance the account. | **ChatGPT Work + document creation + approved CRM connector** | Lead |
| ☐ | Event or conference preparation | Researches attendees and accounts, clusters them by relevance, proposes conversation hooks, and prepares follow-up notes. | Seth decides whom to approach and keeps the interaction human rather than treating the event as a mass campaign. | **Deep Research + file/data analysis + ChatGPT Voice rehearsal** | Breadth |
| ☐ | Open-loop and reply-owed monitoring | Detects promised follow-ups, unanswered threads, blockers, deadlines, and meetings needing preparation. | The system may flag or draft; it does not send messages or create commitments without review. | **Workspace agent + email/calendar/CRM apps + scheduled task** | Proof |

##### 5. RevOps, CRM, and learning loops

| Pick | Workflow | What AI contributes | Human-owned boundary | Modality / OpenAI surface | Answer role |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| ☐ | CRM-ready draft updates | Proposes source-linked updates to account, contact, opportunity, activity, next-step, and qualification fields. | High-impact fields, stage, value, commitments, and customer claims stay review-only. | **Workspace agent + CRM app/connector + ChatGPT Work** | Lead |
| ☐ | CRM hygiene scan | Flags incomplete records, stale next steps, conflicting fields, orphaned contacts, duplicates, and activities that need a real follow-up task. | The owner decides the correction; automation must not “clean” ambiguity into false certainty. | **Codex + CRM export/file analysis or a scoped workspace agent** | Proof |
| ☐ | Lead routing and assignment support | Applies deterministic territory rules first, then summarizes ambiguous strategic cases and suggests a route with reasons. | RevOps owns routing policy and exceptions; AI should not silently reroute high-value accounts. | **Codex for deterministic rules + workspace agent for ambiguous review** | Depth |
| ☐ | Deduplication and branch-parent review | Normalizes domains and names, finds probable duplicates, and separates update records from net-new records. | Ambiguous entity relationships go to a review queue before CRM import. | **Codex + spreadsheet/data analysis** | Proof |
| ☐ | Source, confidence, and freshness ledger | Preserves source URL, verification date, confidence, inference status, and any conflict behind important fields. | Users must be able to inspect and correct the evidence; confidence is not verification. | **Codex + structured files/database + web verification** | Depth |
| ☐ | Pipeline and activity inspection | Summarizes coverage, conversion between stages, follow-up quality, age, and where the motion is breaking. | Managers and sellers diagnose cause; the model does not reduce performance to activity volume alone. | **File/data analysis + CRM connector + charts/visualization** | Depth |
| ☐ | Daily priority brief | Produces a short “top accounts, meetings, replies owed, risks, and recommended actions” view instead of another raw dashboard. | Seth chooses the day and can override the ranking. | **Workspace agent or ChatGPT Work + apps + schedule** | Proof |
| ☐ | Sequence and experiment analysis | Compares replies, meetings, persona, message, segment, signal, channel, and timing to identify useful patterns. | Humans define clean cohorts and avoid claiming causation from noisy sales data. | **File/data analysis + Codex + visualizations** | Depth |
| ☐ | Objection and reply clustering | Groups recurring objections, anxieties, referrals, and reasons for rejection to expose message or qualification gaps. | The team reviews the clusters and decides whether the playbook should change. | **File/data analysis + Chat** | Breadth |
| ☐ | Playbook and rubric codification | Converts repeated wins, losses, questions, and manager feedback into talk tracks, qualification rules, templates, and reusable skills. | Leaders approve the standard; one anecdote should not become policy. | **ChatGPT Work + Codex skills + company knowledge/files** | Lead |
| ☐ | Closed-loop account-intelligence learning | Feeds seller corrections, replies, meetings, disqualifications, and outcomes back into scoring, routing, and QA rules. | Taxonomy changes require inspection; the system must not optimize blindly on incomplete outcomes. | **Workspace agent + CRM/apps + Codex/data analysis** | Depth |
| ☐ | BDR-to-AD and sales-to-delivery continuity | Preserves buyer language, decision history, proof, risk, owners, and accepted next steps so context survives a handoff. | The receiving owner validates the handoff; the agent cannot make commercial or implementation commitments. | **ChatGPT Work + CRM/company-knowledge apps + review-ready files** | Lead |

##### 6. Personal operating system and Chief of Staff layer

| Pick | Workflow                                  | What AI contributes                                                                                                         | Human-owned boundary                                                                                                      | Modality / OpenAI surface                                              | Answer role |
| ---- | ----------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------- | ----------- |
| ☐    | Morning brief                             | Synthesizes calendar, open tasks, reply-owed threads, account risks, and the few priorities that would be costly to miss.   | The brief is read-only decision support; Seth chooses the priorities.                                                     | **Workspace agent or ChatGPT Work + apps/connectors + schedule**       | Proof       |
| ☐    | Inbox and work-channel triage             | Separates urgent customer, partner, manager, and active-deal asks from newsletters, noise, and non-blocking updates.        | Sensitivity, urgency, and response remain human judgments; email and Slack sends require review.                          | **Workspace agent + email/Slack/CRM apps**                             | Breadth     |
| ☐    | Calendar and meeting-memory maintenance   | Links upcoming meetings to account context, stages prebriefs, and attaches postbriefs and next-step summaries afterward.    | A calendar row is not evidence that prep is complete, and a meeting does not create a task unless there is a real action. | **Workspace agent + calendar/meeting/CRM apps + schedule**             | Proof       |
| ☐    | Waiting-on-me / waiting-on-others tracker | Maintains owners, blockers, deadlines, drafts staged, closed loops, and items awaiting approval.                            | The agent must not invent an owner, deadline, or commitment.                                                              | **ChatGPT Work + task/email/calendar apps**                            | Breadth     |
| ☐    | Recurring research digest                 | Monitors selected AI, product, industry, or account topics and creates a sourced review queue or summary.                   | Seth inspects primary sources and decides what is material enough to retain or use.                                       | **Deep Research/web search + ChatGPT Work + scheduled task**           | Breadth     |
| ☐    | Persistent decision and account memory    | Stores source-linked facts, decisions, corrections, and reusable lessons in the appropriate system so context compounds.    | Raw evidence stays immutable; private or informal context is not silently promoted into official CRM truth.               | **Files/company knowledge + Codex or workspace-agent memory**          | Lead        |
| ☐    | Draft staging and approval queue          | Lets bounded workflows prepare research, briefs, responses, and updates for review without taking the consequential action. | Customer-facing messages, pricing, legal terms, CRM-critical fields, and external commitments remain explicitly approved. | **ChatGPT Work or workspace agent + apps/connectors + approval gates** | Lead        |

#### A clean composition rule

A balanced final answer will usually contain:

1. **One research example** — how Seth gets to a sharper account thesis.
2. **One sparring example** — how AI improves judgment rather than replacing it.
3. **One proof example** — how Seth makes a workflow or value case tangible.
4. **Optionally, one RevOps or Chief of Staff example** — how context, follow-through, and CRM hygiene become more reliable.
5. **One closing boundary** — AI gathers, compares, pressure-tests, and drafts; Seth owns truth, relevance, relationship judgment, qualification, customer communication, and commercial commitments.

This structure keeps the identity clear: **enterprise seller first; AI fluency as the force multiplier.**

**Spoken answer — about 60 seconds:**

> I use AI in five modes: to learn, think, build, operate, and sell. I maintain a source-backed second brain; use voice and Work to structure rough ideas; use Codex to build and test software and buyer-specific artifacts; run bounded scheduled workflows for digests and task triage; and use AI for account research, stakeholder hypotheses, call preparation, and CRM-ready draft summaries.
>
> At OpenAI, I would apply that intuition across employee knowledge work, developer productivity, customer operations, and AI embedded in a customer's product. I would not pitch the menu. The breadth helps me form hypotheses; discovery identifies the one workflow with a real owner and measurable value.
>
> In this role, AI would help me synthesize evidence before a meeting, make value tangible during the sales cycle, and preserve next steps afterward. Sources, customer claims, CRM updates, and commercial decisions remain human-reviewed. I am an enterprise seller first; AI fluency makes my research deeper and my point of view more concrete.

**Depth version:**

> I use AI in five modes: to learn, think, build, operate, and sell.
>
> To learn, I maintain a source-backed second brain that turns articles, notes, and transcripts into knowledge I can retrieve. To think, I use voice and long-running work threads to get rough ideas out of my head and turn them into structured plans. To build, I use Codex to create and test software, ROI calculators, workflow diagrams, and small demos. To operate, I use bounded scheduled workflows for daily digests, task triage, and recurring knowledge updates. To sell, I use AI for account research, stakeholder hypotheses, call preparation, follow-up drafts, and CRM-ready draft summaries.
>
> At OpenAI, I would apply that breadth across four enterprise surfaces: employee knowledge work, developer productivity, customer operations, and AI embedded into a customer's own product. I would not pitch all four. The breadth helps me find hypotheses; discovery determines the one workflow worth pursuing.
>
> For the Strategic BDR role specifically, I would use AI before a meeting to synthesize account evidence, during the sales cycle to make the value tangible through a buyer-specific artifact or demo, and after the meeting to preserve decisions and next steps. Sources, customer-facing claims, CRM changes, and commercial decisions would remain human-reviewed.
>
> That is how I see my positioning: I am an enterprise seller first. AI fluency is the differentiator that helps me research more deeply, form better hypotheses, and show a customer what change could look like.

**If asked whether you use autonomous agents or automations:**

> Yes. I use bounded scheduled workflows for things such as a daily AI-industry digest, task and meeting triage, and recurring knowledge ingestion. I let the system collect, summarize, prioritize, and draft. I still inspect the source and approve anything customer-facing, commercial, or consequential. The important question is not whether the workflow sounds technically impressive; it is which repeatable work can be delegated safely and how the result is reviewed.

**Range to remember:** learn → think → build → operate → sell.

**Enterprise translation:** employee knowledge → engineering → customer operations → product/API.

**Source basis:** [[OpenAI-SDR-comprehensive-answer-expansion-bank#Why OpenAI?]], [[OpenAI-SDR-comprehensive-answer-expansion-bank#How Do You Use Codex Daily As Your Main Driver?]], [[OpenAI-SDR-technical-acumen-interview-pack#55. How do you use Codex in practice?]], and the July 22 panel transcript. Personal examples are Seth's own workflow descriptions; they are not customer outcome claims.

### FR-S2. How would you rate your AI fluency?

> [!tip] FINAL-ROUND EDIT 2026-07-26: NEW
> Use the same answer in both packs. The rating is confident, role-calibrated, and explicit about what Seth is not claiming.

> I would put myself at about an eight out of ten for this role.
>
> I am strong enough to explain concepts such as RAG, agents, and evals in plain language, test products myself, build useful workflows, and translate what I learn into a customer use case, risk, and proof step. I use the tools every day rather than learning them only for the interview.
>
> I would not say ten because the frontier changes constantly, and I am not claiming to be a research scientist or the engineer who should make every production architecture decision. My edge is seller-level AI intuition: I can understand enough to diagnose the workflow, ask better questions, make the value tangible, and know when to bring in a technical specialist.
>
> I keep improving by testing new capabilities on real tasks, comparing outputs on the same workload, reading primary product material, and forcing myself to explain the result at both executive and technical-buyer altitude.

**Why eight:** daily use + practical builds + plain-language explanation + commercial translation.

**Why not ten:** honest boundary around research depth, production engineering, and a rapidly changing frontier.

### FR-S3. What would your first 30 days targeting a Fortune 500 account look like?

> [!tip] FINAL-ROUND EDIT 2026-07-26: NEW
> This is an account-specific 30-day plan, not the onboarding 30/60/90 answer in Q112. Its structure is adapted from the sponsor-backed PoC path in [[OpenAI-SDR-DoorDash-Loom-final-canonical-document#Question: If you had 30 days to build momentum, what would you do?]].

> My goal after 30 days would not be to claim I had cracked a Fortune 500 account. It would be to turn a broad logo into one evidence-backed workflow, a credible champion path, and an agreed next proof step.
>
> In days 1 to 10, I would align with the Account Director and inspect the internal account history before doing external research. I would map the company's strategic priorities, current AI estate, available and permitted product or usage signals, likely workflow owners, existing vendors, and relevant OpenAI proof. I would generate several problem hypotheses, rank them by value, urgency, access, and proof proximity, and produce a one-page account thesis and initial stakeholder map.
>
> In days 11 to 20, I would test the thesis with the people closest to the work. My outreach would be specific to the workflow and stakeholder, not a generic AI pitch. In discovery, I would establish the current process, measurable friction, executive priority, current solution, adoption gap, and what they have already tried. If the evidence supports it, I would select one wedge with a potential champion and define the baseline and success criteria. A lightweight demo or workflow diagram would be proof of preparation, not a claim that I had built their production solution.
>
> In days 21 to 30, I would help the champion widen the process. I would bring in the economic, technical, security, data, and operational stakeholders needed to evaluate the idea; resolve the biggest feasibility and governance questions; and co-author a short business case. The desired output is a sponsor-backed discovery or PoC plan with a named owner, bounded scope, approved data path, success metric, decision process, and next meeting. If those conditions are absent, the honest output is a documented nurture or disqualification decision.

| Period | Objective | Required output |
|---|---|---|
| Days 1–10 | Align internally and form ranked workflow hypotheses | One-page account thesis, evidence ledger, and stakeholder map |
| Days 11–20 | Validate the problem and identify a champion | Customer-confirmed workflow, baseline, success measure, and champion test |
| Days 21–30 | Multi-thread and earn the next proof step | Sponsor-backed discovery or PoC path, or an evidence-based stop decision |

**Source basis:** The July 22 Fortune 500 answer supplied the workflow-ranking, stakeholder-mapping, adjacent-proof, and prototype ideas. The DoorDash Loom supplied the champion-led progression from operational discovery to technical/security validation and a sponsor-backed PoC path.

### FR-S4. What skill are you actively leveling up?

> [!tip] FINAL-ROUND EDIT 2026-07-26: NEW
> This keeps the useful part of the July 22 answer without presenting Seth as a nontechnical candidate trying to become an engineer.

> The skill I am actively leveling up is translating fast-moving AI capability into a short, commercially useful explanation for an executive.
>
> I am naturally curious and can go deep into how a workflow works. The risk is answering with everything I know when the buyer first needs the definition, the business consequence, and the next proof step.
>
> I am working on that in a deliberate loop. I test new capabilities on real tasks, read the primary material, build a small workflow when that is the fastest way to understand it, and then force myself to explain it at three altitudes: one sentence for an executive, thirty seconds for a business buyer, and a deeper version for a technical evaluator. After important conversations, I review where I went too deep or answered the wrong layer and rewrite the opening.
>
> The goal is not to become the implementation engineer. It is to become a better enterprise AI seller: technically credible enough to diagnose the workflow and commercially disciplined enough to make the decision clear.

**Values demonstrated:** curiosity, humility, feedback-seeking, disciplined practice, and customer-centered communication.

## Source Precedence

1. [[OpenAI-SDR-recruiter-screen-learnings-and-interview-strategy-2026-07-11]] defines the official round and its evaluation focus.
2. Seth's direct corrections dated 2026-07-20 and later control all career and commercial facts.
3. [[OpenAI-SDR-hiring-leader-interview-debrief-2026-07-17]] defines answer-control and credibility repairs; its older unreconciled metrics do not override later corrections.
4. [[OpenAI-SDR-comprehensive-answer-expansion-bank]] and [[OpenAI-SDR-sales-acumen-interview-pack]] supply candidate language and examples.
5. Raw transcripts preserve what was actually asked or advised. Friend/process intelligence remains unofficial.
6. Second Brain sales material supplies methodology, not facts about Seth's employment, quota, customers or results.

## Drafting Contract

Every completed entry should follow this format:

### N. Interview question

> A concise, speakable answer. Lead with the literal answer, then explain the reasoning. The default length is 30–60 seconds unless the question explicitly asks for a deal walkthrough.

**How the answer works:**

1. State the answer directly.
2. Explain the operating logic.
3. Prove it with one specific example.
4. Land one verified result or learning.

**Simple example:**

> One short example the interviewer can remember.

**Raw-source basis:** Identify the raw transcript, user-confirmed correction or source capture. Distinguish verified fact, inference and placeholder. Do not cite a compiled answer as if it were raw evidence.

## I. Sales Identity And Credibility

### 1. Tell me about yourself and walk me through your career.

> [!tip] FINAL-ROUND EDIT 2026-07-26: TRUTH REPAIR + IDENTITY ANCHOR
> Use this before the AI-fluency answers. It leads with enterprise sales outcomes and keeps AI as the differentiator.

> Yeah. The simplest way to explain my background is that I’m a full-cycle seller who became more technical over time.
>
> I started at Boxo as the first business hire when they were in the seed stage, and my time there had two clear phases. In my first year as an enterprise SDR, I built the outbound motion across APAC and sourced and qualified strategic opportunities with Vodafone, Binance, Grab, UnionBank of the Philippines, CaixaBank, Maya Bank, Zalo, GCash, DANA, Paytm, TrueMoney and Touch ’n Go. My quota was approximately four SQOs per quarter, and I averaged approximately six, or about 150% attainment. At approximately USD 200K per SQO, that represented approximately USD 4.8M in annualized qualified pipeline.
>
> In my second year, I progressed into a self-sourcing, full-cycle AE role. I owned discovery, stakeholder management, technical validation, negotiation and close. UNA Financial began at approximately USD 200K in ARR and Epon was approximately USD 300K, giving me approximately USD 500K in initial combined ARR. UNA later expanded to approximately USD 300K, taking the combined portfolio to approximately USD 600K.
>
> I then joined Airwallex, a series F company because I wanted to prove I could perform in a more mature quota environment. As the second outbound hire in Singapore, I owned new business without SDR support. In one year, I generated approximately USD 1M in gross profit against an individual annual quota of approximately USD 500K, or about 200% attainment. That included 22 Tier 1 accounts inside my formal territory.
>
> Since then, through my own AI companies, I’ve continued running the same b2b full-cycle motion: sourcing customers, running discovery, scoping solutions, writing proposals, negotiating and closing. That work has also made me much more fluent in the AI workflows customers are trying to adopt.
>
> So the progression is quite clear to me: Boxo taught me enterprise complexity, Airwallex taught me quota discipline and repeatability, and the founder work added technical depth. That combination is why this OpenAI role makes sense—I can create strategic opportunities, navigate complex buying groups and make a technical product commercially relevant.

**How the answer works:**

1. Opens with the literal identity: full-cycle seller of technical products.
2. Uses Boxo to prove enterprise complexity and end-to-end ownership.
3. Uses Airwallex to prove performance in a formal quota environment.
4. Frames founder work as continued selling plus greater AI fluency.
5. Ends with the commercial value Seth would bring to OpenAI.

**Short version if interrupted:**

> I am a full-cycle seller who became more technical over time. Boxo taught me complex enterprise selling, Airwallex proved I could execute repeatedly against a formal quota, and my founder work kept me close to customers while deepening my AI fluency. Across those chapters, the consistent work has been creating opportunities, navigating buying groups and moving technical products toward a commercial decision.

**Delivery notes:**

- Target approximately 65–75 seconds at a calm speaking pace; do not rush the metrics.
- Pause after Boxo and Airwallex so the interviewer can interrupt or drill down.
- Use “I” for personal ownership and avoid detouring into Cambridge, the OpenAI grant, tool chronology, Sample Healthcare or individual AI builds unless asked.
- If challenged on the seller-versus-builder issue, stop and use Question 3 rather than extending this answer.

**Raw-source basis:** The July 17 interview transcript (R1) shows that the previous career recap over-weighted education, AI tools and project chronology, which caused the interviewer to redirect toward sales. Commercial figures and role definitions use Seth's corrections dated 2026-07-20 as compiled in C1. C2 supplied the original sales-led structure but its obsolete USD 450K Boxo figure was rejected. QMD retrieval added the candidate-ownership principle from S2 and the founder-proxy commercial framing from S3; those sources shaped the structure but were not used as evidence for Seth's career facts.

### 2. Why do you genuinely like sales?

> I genuinely like sales because it combines investigation, human judgment and the chance to create conviction around a better future.
>
> The part I enjoy most is creating something from nothing. You start with an account that has not asked to speak to you, work out what is changing in the business, who actually cares and whether there is a real reason to act. Then you earn the conversation and help the buyer turn an unclear problem into a decision they can defend internally.
>
> I also like how the learning compounds. At Airwallex, once I understood the travel segment properly, every conversation gave me better language, sharper objections, new stakeholder clues and stronger proof for the next account.
>
> And I like that sales has real stakes. You can have a smart strategy and a great-looking pipeline, but eventually you have to produce a result. There is skin in the game: your judgment, preparation and execution all have consequences. I enjoy that pressure and the competitive side of trying to outperform.
>
> At the simplest level, I enjoy selling a vision, seeing someone recognize a better future state and helping them move toward it.

**How the answer works:**

1. Opens with the combination Seth genuinely enjoys: investigation, judgment and conviction building.
2. Connects that motivation directly to the SDR craft of creating an opportunity from an unprompted account.
3. Uses Airwallex's travel segment to show how customer conversations compound into better selling judgment.
4. Makes the competitive motivation explicit: sales has real stakes and a measurable result.
5. Closes on Seth's own language about selling a vision and helping someone see a better future state.

**Short version if interrupted:**

> I like sales because you create something from nothing. You investigate an account, develop a point of view, earn a conversation and help the buyer reach a decision they can defend internally. I also enjoy that there is real skin in the game: eventually your judgment and execution have to produce a result. At the simplest level, I like selling a vision and helping someone recognize a better future state.

**Delivery notes:**

- Target approximately 45–55 seconds; let the interviewer feel the intrinsic motivation before adding the Airwallex example.
- Put slight emphasis on “creating something from nothing” and “real stakes”; those phrases make the answer specific to sales.
- Keep the competitive point matter-of-fact. Do not turn it into a speech about hustle or pressure.
- If challenged on whether building is actually the stronger motivation, move directly to Question 3.

**Raw-source basis:** R1 contains Seth's direct statement that sales remains his primary interest and that the part giving him the most joy is selling a vision and seeing people recognize how the future could be. C1 provides the account-creation, compounding-learning and accountability language, including the Airwallex travel-segment example. C2 supplied the concise buyer-conviction frame. QMD retrieval from S1 and S4 added the distinction between a pleasant conversation and genuine buyer movement; those sources shaped the answer but do not serve as evidence for Seth's personal motivation.

### 3. Are you really a seller or a builder?

> I’m a seller. Building is a capability I developed because I wanted to sell technical products more credibly.
>
> The clearest evidence is what I have repeatedly chosen to own. At Boxo and Airwallex, I selected accounts, prospected, ran discovery, navigated stakeholders, negotiated and closed. In my own AI companies, even when I built the solution, I was still the person finding the customer, diagnosing the workflow, writing the proposal and winning the work.
>
> The technical depth makes me better commercially. I can understand what the customer is actually trying to change, ask better questions, communicate credibly with product and engineering, and avoid promising something that will fail during implementation.
>
> In this role, I would apply that fluency to account strategy, discovery and opportunity development. The accountability I want is commercial. The cleanest description of me is a technically fluent seller.

**How the answer works:**

1. Answers the identity question immediately rather than defending the career chronology.
2. Proves the identity through the commercial activities Seth has repeatedly chosen to own.
3. Treats founder building as continued customer acquisition and full-cycle selling.
4. Converts technical depth into better diagnosis, buyer credibility and implementation judgment.
5. Ends with the precise identity the interviewer should remember: technically fluent seller with commercial accountability.

**Short version if interrupted:**

> I’m a seller. Building is a capability I developed to sell technical products more credibly. Across Boxo, Airwallex and my own AI companies, I have repeatedly chosen to own the commercial work: finding customers, running discovery, navigating stakeholders, negotiating and winning the business. The technical depth helps me ask better questions and avoid overpromising, but the accountability I want is commercial.

**Delivery notes:**

- Say “I’m a seller” without hesitation or qualification.
- Keep the proof behavioral: what Seth repeatedly owned, rather than what title he held.
- Do not launch into a technical-project example unless the interviewer asks how the fluency improves sales execution.
- Keep the role boundary clear: the fluency supports account strategy and opportunity development.

**Raw-source basis:** R1 contains both the builder-versus-seller objection and Seth's direct explanation that he became more technical to strengthen his ability to sell. C1 and C2 establish repeated full-cycle ownership across Boxo, Airwallex and founder work. QMD retrieval from S1 and S3 shaped the commercial interpretation: technical understanding improves discovery, proof and implementation credibility, while founder work collects buyer language and objections that strengthen the sales motion. Those QMD sources do not establish Seth's career facts.

### 4. Why OpenAI?

> There are three reasons OpenAI is specific for me.
>
> First, I already use the product as an operating layer for my own work. I use Codex to build software, run prospecting workflows, maintain my Second Brain and deliver AI projects. So my conviction comes from experiencing how the technology changes the way I work.
>
> What still feels remarkable is the pace. I was using GPT-4 back in 2023. GPT-5.4 arrived on March 5 this year, and GPT-5.6 followed on July 9—just over four months later. Experiencing that acceleration in the work itself, rather than watching the category from the outside, is a big part of why I want to be close to the frontier.

I would enjoy helping people and organizations benefit from this transformation. 
>
> Second, I find the enterprise sales problem unusually interesting. OpenAI’s product surface spans everyday knowledge work, software development, agents and strategic enterprise deployments. That creates a happy problem: almost every enterprise can imagine a use case. The real commercial work is identifying which workflow matters, who owns it, what proof the customer needs and how to turn experimentation into measurable adoption. That is exactly the kind of selling I want to master.
>
> Third, the timing in Singapore is compelling. OpenAI has established its first Applied AI Lab outside the US here and is making Singapore one of its Forward-Deployed Engineering hubs. Having sold technical products across APAC, the opportunity to help build that local enterprise motion feels unusually aligned.
>
> So this is more specific than wanting to work in AI. I want to help OpenAI translate technology I already use and believe in into real enterprise adoption across a region I understand.

**How the answer works:**

1. Establishes product conviction through lived Codex usage and firsthand experience of the model progression rather than generic enthusiasm.
2. Defines the enterprise sales challenge as workflow, owner, proof and adoption selection.
3. Makes OpenAI specific through the breadth of its current enterprise product and deployment surface.
4. Makes the timing specific through the verified Singapore Applied AI Lab and Forward-Deployed Engineering hub.
5. Connects Seth's APAC enterprise-sales background directly to the commercial opportunity.

**Short version if interrupted:**

> OpenAI is specific for me because I already use Codex as an operating layer for my work, so the product conviction is lived. Commercially, I am excited by the challenge of turning a very broad product surface into one owned workflow, clear proof and measurable enterprise adoption. And with OpenAI building its first Applied AI Lab outside the US in Singapore, the timing is unusually aligned with my experience selling technical products across APAC.

**Delivery notes:**

- Target approximately 60–75 seconds; this answer can run longer than a normal rapid-fire response because the recruiter explicitly expects a substantive “why OpenAI.”
- Keep the personal-use examples to one sentence. The answer should land on the enterprise sales problem, not become a Codex feature tour.
- Deliver the GPT-4-to-GPT-5.6 progression as genuine astonishment, not as a model-history recital or benchmark claim.
- Say “happy problem” lightly; the important point is the discipline of choosing the right workflow and proof.
- Do not volunteer volatile adoption figures, product availability or customer claims.
- If asked why OpenAI over another lab, answer the comparison directly rather than stretching this response.

**Raw-source basis:** C2 supplies Seth's direct Codex usage, the “almost every enterprise could be an ICP” insight and the workflow-owner-proof-adoption framing. The recruiter screen establishes that genuine AI curiosity and an original point of view are explicit evaluation criteria. QMD retrieval from `Seth Second Brain/wiki/gtm-sales/openai-enterprise-product-framing.md` shaped the ascending enterprise-value logic. [OpenAI announced GPT-4 on March 14, 2023](https://openai.com/index/gpt-4-research/); the official [OpenAI API changelog](https://developers.openai.com/api/docs/changelog) records GPT-5.4 on March 5, 2026 and GPT-5.6 on July 9, 2026, just over four months apart. Current company claims were verified on 2026-07-21 against OpenAI's official enterprise pages, its June 2026 “Built to benefit everyone” plan and the May 19, 2026 “Introducing OpenAI for Singapore” announcement. Product availability, adoption figures and customer claims were deliberately omitted from the spoken answer.

### 5. Why this Strategic BDR role rather than an AE role?

> This is a deliberate career decision. I have closing experience, but title continuity is not what I am optimizing for. I am optimizing for the product, the quality of the accounts and the enterprise-sales environment in which I can compound fastest.
>
> I also understand the role boundary. The Account Director owns the formal close. My job is to identify the right accounts, create a relevant entry, run early discovery, map the buying group and develop the account until there is a qualified opportunity worth the AD’s time.
>
> My closing experience helps because I understand what the opportunity will need downstream. I can distinguish curiosity from a real business priority, identify the missing stakeholders and proof, and give the AD a clear account thesis rather than simply a meeting on the calendar.
>
> I genuinely enjoy that part of selling, and this role lets me perform it on strategic enterprise accounts while learning a demanding enterprise-AI motion. My focus would be to excel in this role, become someone the Account Directors trust with their accounts and earn broader scope through performance. I would not assume a fixed promotion timeline.

**How the answer works:**

1. Frames the move as a deliberate optimization for product, accounts and selling environment.
2. Accepts the actual role boundary: the Account Director owns the formal close.
3. Defines Strategic BDR ownership as account selection, entry, discovery, stakeholder mapping and opportunity development.
4. Converts prior closing experience into better upstream qualification and AD handoff quality.
5. Commits to earning trust and broader scope through performance without assuming a promotion timeline.

**Short version if interrupted:**

> This is deliberate. I have closed before, but I am optimizing for the product, the strategic accounts and the enterprise-sales environment rather than title continuity. I understand that the AD owns the formal close. My role is to create and develop a high-conviction opportunity, and my closing experience helps me recognize what the account will need downstream. I want to excel at that work, earn the ADs’ trust and let broader scope follow performance.

**Delivery notes:**

- Do not describe the role as a temporary step or imply that the work is beneath Seth's experience.
- State the closing boundary clearly so the interviewer knows Seth understands the job.
- Emphasize immediate value to the Account Director before discussing learning or progression.
- Never repeat the recruiter's 12–18-month indication or the hiring leader's five-to-seven-year comment as a promise or forecast.
- If pressed on title, return to the quality of the work, accounts, team and learning environment.

**Raw-source basis:** R1 states that OpenAI is deliberately hiring full-cycle sellers into this role and that the Strategic BDR sources and develops opportunities while the Account Director owns the formal close. R2 reports that candidates are explicitly tested on their willingness to take a step back. The canonical recruiter strategy describes a senior founding inside-sales team working with strategic ADs on large, multi-threaded accounts. C1 and the official JD provide the deliberate-title and account-strategy framing. ### 12. What does value-based selling mean to you?

> Value-based selling means I begin with the customer's problem, its business consequence, the outcome they want and why action matters now. I do not lead with features, price or my own target. I establish the current state, quantify the gap where the evidence allows it and connect the smallest credible scope or proof to the result the buyer needs.
>
> At EU Holidays, the value was not simply a better FX rate. It was payment reliability, easier reconciliation, operational continuity and confidence across the corridors that mattered to the business. A controlled volume commitment made the decision safer while giving us evidence for broader adoption.

**What this answer must prove:** Seth sells against customer outcomes and status-quo cost rather than feature superiority or discounting.

**Primary example:** UNA expansion case or EU Holidays reliability and corridor case.

> [!quote] Buyer-centered sales principles
> 2. “Sales is about people and it's about problem solving. It is not about solutions or technology or chemicals or lines of code or artichokes. It's about people and it's about solving problems.”
>
> 6. “Being valuable and useful is all you ever need to do to sell things. Help people out. Send interesting posts. Write birthday cards. Record videos sharing your ideas for growing their business. Introduce people who would benefit from knowing each other then get out of the way, expecting nothing in return. Do this consistently and authentically and people will find ways to give you money. I promise.”
>
> 7. “No one cares about your quota, your payroll, your opex, your burn rate, etc. No one. They care about the problem you are solving for them.”

| Stage                                           |                   Monthly |           Weekly |

**Conversion shorthand:** 80% show rate · 60% held → SQL · 45% held → SQO · 25% held → activation · 56% SQO → activation.

Each month, I actively worked roughly 120 Tier 1 and Tier 2 accounts. That produced about 20 first meetings.

Around 12 became SQLs, meaning the first conversation confirmed that it was the right account, there was a real business problem, and there was a credible reason to continue.

Around nine became SQOs. That meant we had gone beyond identifying a problem: we had confirmed an owner, meaningful potential value, a realistic buying path, the relevant stakeholders and an agreed next step. In plain English, an SQL was worth investigating; an SQO was a real opportunity worth investing in.

Around seven reached proposal and five went live—two Tier 1 and three Tier 2. A separate scaled motion produced another six Tier 3 activations, giving me an operating target of 11 activations against a formal floor of 10.
