### 114. Boxo: How did you prospect, manage your cadence and create qualified enterprise pipeline?

> Boxo was a named-account enterprise motion, but I still managed it through a disciplined weekly prospecting cadence.
>
> I worked backward from a monthly target of approximately 20 qualified first meetings scheduled. Around 16 would normally be held, five would become SQLs after confirming a real initiative, consequence and timing, and approximately two would become SQOs after we validated the owner, potential value, wider stakeholder path and credible next step.
>
> That translated into roughly five first meetings booked and four held each week, producing one or two SQLs and approximately one SQO every two weeks. Across the year, the operating model was approximately 24 SQOs and about USD 4.8M in sourced qualified pipeline. Against a quota of approximately four SQOs per quarter, or 16 annually, that was about 150% attainment.
>
> The most important part of the upstream work was identifying the real owner. These companies had large and complicated organizations, so I could not assume the same title owned the initiative in every account. Sometimes it was the Head of Partnerships, sometimes a Product owner, sometimes Engineering, and the easiest accounts were those with a dedicated mini-app team.
>
> Before outreach, I researched the company's platform strategy, active expansion signals and likely buying committee. I then mapped the stakeholders who might own the business initiative, product experience, technical evaluation and executive decision. My initial outreach went primarily through LinkedIn and email, with the message tailored to the responsibility that person appeared to own.
>
> For UNA, the active eSIM initiative pointed me toward the Head of Partnerships. In another account, the right entry point might be Product because it owned the customer experience, or Engineering because the company was actively evaluating whether to build a mini-app layer internally.
>
> I ran a rolling outreach pool rather than repeatedly messaging the same small group. My weekly rhythm was broadly similar to Airwallex: approximately 200 buyer-facing touches across LinkedIn and email, or around 40 per working day, combining new outreach with appropriately spaced follow-ups. The activity number was an input, not the goal. The goal was qualified buyer engagement.
>
> I also used travel strategically. For a cluster of high-priority accounts in another market, I would tell the relevant stakeholders that I was planning to be in their city and ask whether it would be useful to meet. Once enough priority meetings were confirmed, I would book the flight and structure the trip around those conversations. That was particularly effective in APAC, where an in-person meeting could build significantly more trust than another remote introduction.
>
> The quality of the motion is visible in the accounts I qualified. Twelve of the most notable SQOs included major platforms, banks and e-wallets: Vodafone, Binance, Grab, UnionBank of the Philippines, CaixaBank, Maya Bank, Zalo, GCash, DANA, Paytm, TrueMoney and Touch 'n Go.
>
> Every week, I inspected where the funnel was breaking. If an account did not engage, I reviewed whether I had selected the wrong stakeholder or brought a weak point of view. If a first meeting did not qualify, I inspected account fit and urgency. If an SQL did not become an SQO, I looked for a missing owner, economic value, stakeholder path or customer-owned next step.
>
> That is how I think about strategic prospecting: consistent activity creates enough surface area, but account research, stakeholder judgment and qualification discipline determine whether that activity becomes valuable pipeline.

**Monthly and weekly operating rhythm:**

| Metric | Monthly | Weekly planning equivalent |
|---|---:|---:|
| First meetings scheduled | Approximately 20 | Approximately 5 |
| First meetings held | Approximately 16 | Approximately 4 |
| SQLs created | Approximately 5 | Approximately 1–2 |
| SQOs created | Approximately 2 | Approximately 1 every two weeks |
| Buyer-facing touches | Approximately 800 | Approximately 200 |
| Formal SQO quota | Approximately 1.3 monthly equivalent | 4 per quarter / 16 annually |
| SQO quota attainment | — | Approximately 150% |
| Average value per SQO | Approximately USD 200K | Approximately USD 200K |
| Formal qualified-pipeline quota | Approximately USD 267K monthly equivalent | Approximately USD 3.2M annually |
| Sourced qualified pipeline produced | Approximately USD 400K average per month | Approximately USD 4.8M annually |

**Annualized funnel:**

| Metric                        | Annualized operating model |
| ----------------------------- | -------------------------: |
| First meetings scheduled      |          Approximately 240 |
| First meetings held           |          Approximately 192 |
| SQLs                          |           Approximately 60 |
| SQOs                          |           Approximately 24 |
| Formal SQO quota              |           Approximately 16 |
| SQO quota attainment          |         Approximately 150% |
| Most notable named SQOs       |                         12 |
| Formal qualified-pipeline quota |     Approximately USD 3.2M |
| Sourced qualified pipeline produced | Approximately USD 4.8M |
| Average value per SQO         |     Approximately USD 200K |

**Simple conversion math:**

| Conversion | Result | Speakable phrasing |
|---|---:|---|
| Scheduled → held | 16 ÷ 20 = 80% | Four out of five scheduled meetings held |
| Held → SQL | 5 ÷ 16 = 31% | Approximately one in three first meetings qualified |
| SQL → SQO | 2 ÷ 5 = 40% | Two in five SQLs became qualified opportunities |
| Held → SQO | 2 ÷ 16 = 12.5% | Approximately one in eight held meetings became an SQO |

**How I found the right stakeholder:**

1. Identify the account's active platform or category-expansion initiative.
2. Map the likely business owner, Product owner, Engineering evaluator and executive decision-maker.
3. Look for a dedicated mini-app or ecosystem team first, where one existed.
4. Tailor the LinkedIn or email message to the outcome that stakeholder owned.
5. Use discovery to confirm whether the initial contact was the real owner.
6. Multi-thread into the next stakeholder required to validate the opportunity.

**Twelve notable qualified opportunities:**

| Category | Accounts |
|---|---|
| Major platforms | Vodafone, Binance, Grab |
| Banks | UnionBank of the Philippines, CaixaBank, Maya Bank |
| Major e-wallets | Zalo, GCash, DANA, Paytm, TrueMoney, Touch 'n Go |

**Shorter version:**

> At Boxo, I worked backward from approximately 20 first meetings scheduled per month. Around 16 held, five became SQLs and approximately two became SQOs. My quota was approximately four SQOs per quarter, and I averaged approximately six, or about 150% attainment. Across the year, that represented approximately 24 qualified opportunities and about USD 4.8M in sourced qualified pipeline at an average of approximately USD 200K per SQO.
>
> The hard part was identifying the real initiative owner. Depending on the company, that could be Partnerships, Product, Engineering or a dedicated mini-app team. I used LinkedIn and email primarily, tailored the message to that stakeholder's responsibility and then multi-threaded as the decision developed.
>
> Twelve of my most notable qualified opportunities included Vodafone, Binance, Grab, UnionBank of the Philippines, CaixaBank, Maya Bank, Zalo, GCash, DANA, Paytm, TrueMoney and Touch 'n Go.
>
> My operating principle was consistent activity combined with precise stakeholder judgment: enough outreach to create opportunities, but enough account research to make those opportunities worth pursuing.

On 2026-07-28, Seth corrected the average SQO value to approximately USD 200K and recalled approximately 150% quota attainment. That produces a model of four SQOs and approximately USD 800K in pipeline quota per quarter versus six SQOs and approximately USD 1.2M produced, or approximately 16 SQOs / USD 3.2M quota versus 24 SQOs / USD 4.8M produced annually. Seth also confirmed the 12 named SQOs. The LinkedIn/email motion, variable initiative owner and travel-clustering strategy were confirmed on 2026-07-21. The weekly and annual figures are arithmetic planning equivalents rather than a CRM export.

Across approximately 24 SQOs, an average opportunity value of approximately USD 200K produces approximately USD 4.8M in annualized qualified pipeline.

### 115. Boxo/UNA: How did you define the territory, ICP and anti-ICP?

> Boxo was a finite strategic-account motion across APAC. I was not targeting every company with a mobile app.
>
> My first scale screen was approximately ten million downloads across the major app stores. Within that universe, I prioritized banks, fintechs, e-wallets, exchanges, marketplaces and consumer platforms that already had a wallet or payment relationship, meaningful logged-in distribution and an ambition to add multiple third-party services.
>
> The strongest accounts had three additional characteristics. First, they wanted to expand beyond their original product category. Second, adding each new service through a bespoke native integration would create material product and engineering work. Third, there was an identifiable commercial, partnerships or product owner responsible for making the expansion happen.
>
> I treated accounts with the scale, platform characteristics and an active expansion initiative as Tier 1. They received bespoke research, persona-specific outreach and deliberate multi-threading. Structurally attractive accounts without current timing remained Tier 2 and were monitored for signals. Smaller, single-purpose apps without a wallet, ecosystem ambition or credible partner-supply problem were deprioritized.
>
> That kept the territory focused on platforms where Boxo could change expansion speed, monetization or engagement—not merely improve a feature.

**Territory model:**

| Priority | Account characteristics | Seller motion |
|---|---|---|
| Tier 1 | Approximately 10M+ downloads, wallet or payment layer, multi-category ambition and active expansion initiative | Bespoke research, one-to-one outreach and early stakeholder mapping |
| Tier 2 | Strong structural fit but no confirmed priority, owner or timing | Targeted nurture, signal monitoring and selective outreach |
| Deprioritized | Insufficient scale, single-purpose product, no payment layer or no ecosystem ambition | Preserve time until the account changes |

**Raw-source basis:** The approximately 10M-download screen, platform categories, wallet/payment requirement, multi-vertical ambition and anti-ICP come from C1 and C2.

### 116. Boxo/UNA: Why did UNA deserve priority?

> Scale made UNA eligible, but timing and strategic value made it a priority.
>
> UNA fit the structural ICP because it was a scaled fintech with an existing consumer relationship, a payment-adjacent product and an ambition to add more services inside its app. The immediate signal was its intention to expand into eSIMs.
>
> That signal gave me three things I could act on: a current business initiative, a likely owner in Partnerships and a concrete category where Boxo could contribute. It also created a credible route to a larger platform relationship because eSIMs could become the first proof point for adding other merchant categories through the same infrastructure.
>
> UNA also had strategic proof value for Boxo. We were still a young company, so successfully winning and implementing a serious fintech customer could strengthen future customer conversations and establish that the platform could survive enterprise scrutiny.
>
> Compared with another large fintech that had no active initiative or accountable owner, UNA deserved deeper research because it combined fit, timing, access, expansion potential and reference value.

**Why UNA ranked highly:**

1. **Account fit:** Scaled fintech with meaningful consumer distribution.
2. **Product fit:** Wallet or payment-adjacent relationship and multi-service ambition.
3. **Timing:** Active eSIM expansion initiative.
4. **Owner:** Identifiable Head of Partnerships.
5. **Expansion potential:** A first category could lead to more merchant services.
6. **Strategic value:** Important early enterprise proof for Boxo.

**Raw-source basis:** UNA's eSIM expansion signal, platform fit and strategic proof value come from C1 and Seth's reconciled account history.

### 117. Boxo/UNA: What research produced your point of view?

> I treated UNA's eSIM announcement as a routing signal, not as the insight itself.
>
> Before contacting UNA, I researched the eSIM category, identified relevant providers and spoke with potential supply partners. That helped me understand what would be required to assemble the merchant side of the launch rather than approaching UNA with only a generic SDK pitch.
>
> The resulting point of view was that UNA did not merely need another technology vendor. It needed a credible way to source the relevant partners, launch the first category and create reusable infrastructure for additional embedded services.
>
> My outreach could therefore contribute something useful: I understood the initiative they were pursuing, had already investigated the relevant supply side and could connect that immediate requirement to a broader launch model.
>
> The principle I took from that is that a public announcement tells me where to investigate. The value comes from doing enough work behind the signal to help the buyer move the initiative forward.

**Research progression:**

1. UNA publicly signaled an eSIM initiative.
2. I investigated relevant eSIM providers.
3. I spoke with potential supply-side partners.
4. I mapped how Boxo could support both partner access and integration.
5. I converted that work into a specific commercial hypothesis.

**Raw-source basis:** The public eSIM signal and supplier-side research are transcript-backed and appear consistently in R1, R2 and C1.

### 118. Boxo/UNA: Who did you target first, and how did you earn attention?

> I targeted UNA's Head of Partnerships through LinkedIn because that person owned the immediate problem: finding and launching the partners required for the eSIM initiative.
>
> I did not lead with a broad explanation of Boxo's SDK. I led with the fact that I understood the category they were entering and had already spoken with relevant eSIM providers. That made the conversation about helping them advance a current initiative rather than asking them to evaluate an unfamiliar platform in the abstract.
>
> Once the Head of Partnerships engaged, I treated that person as the initial champion. From there, I widened the conversation into Product and Engineering to validate the experience and integration model, then into legal, compliance and risk to resolve enterprise concerns, and finally to the CEO for strategic and commercial approval.
>
> The lesson was that the best first stakeholder is usually the person accountable for the active initiative. Useful work earns the first conversation; the champion then helps the seller reach the people required for the final decision.

**Stakeholder-entry logic:**

- **Channel:** LinkedIn.
- **First stakeholder:** Head of Partnerships.
- **Why that stakeholder:** Owned the eSIM partner initiative.
- **Reason to respond:** Relevant provider research and a credible launch hypothesis.
- **Expansion path:** Partnerships → Product and Engineering → legal/compliance/risk → CEO.

> [!warning] Truth boundary
> The exact number and timing of LinkedIn follow-ups are not remembered and will not be invented.

### 119. Boxo/UNA: How did you move the account forward stage by stage?

> The sales cycle took approximately six months because we had to align a distributed technical and commercial buying group. I did not measure progress only by whether another meeting had been booked. At each stage, I identified the next decision risk, the stakeholder who owned it and the evidence required to move forward.
>
> The first stage was account research. UNA's eSIM initiative gave me the signal, and the supplier conversations gave me a useful reason to approach the Head of Partnerships.
>
> The next stage was business discovery. We validated that this was an active launch priority and that UNA wanted a repeatable way to add third-party services without treating every category as a completely bespoke project.
>
> From there, I expanded into Product and Engineering. The objective was to validate the customer experience, integration model, technical feasibility, support ownership and the comparison with building internally.
>
> Once the technical path was credible, the account moved into enterprise-risk and executive alignment. We worked through legal, compliance, vendor-maturity and responsibility questions, completed a lightweight RFP and connected the decision to UNA's wider platform-expansion goals.
>
> The final pre-signature stage was commercial approval. There was no major pricing objection. The economic comparison was primarily Boxo's platform and support model versus hiring and maintaining sufficient engineering capacity internally. The CEO made the final decision.
>
> After signature, the account moved from eSIMs into e-commerce and additional merchant onboarding. The agreement targeted approximately 100 merchant launches within the first year, and UNA achieved that target.

**Stage progression:**

| Stage | Missing evidence | Advancement milestone |
|---|---|---|
| Research | Was the signal connected to a real initiative? | Supplier research produced a useful account thesis |
| Initial engagement | Was there an accountable business owner? | Head of Partnerships engaged through LinkedIn |
| Discovery | Was the initiative important and actionable? | eSIM launch and reusable-platform need validated |
| Product and technical validation | Could the platform work, and who owned implementation? | Product and Engineering reached a supportable approach |
| Risk and procurement | Could a young vendor pass enterprise scrutiny? | Lightweight RFP and legal/compliance review progressed |
| Commercial approval | Was buying preferable to internal engineering investment? | Commercial case approved by the CEO |
| Adoption and expansion | Could the first use case become a repeatable platform relationship? | E-commerce expansion and approximately 100 merchant launches |

> [!warning] Truth boundary
> This is the verified stage sequence reconstructed across an approximately six-month cycle. It is not presented as a week-by-week CRM history.

### 120. Boxo/UNA: What did discovery uncover?

> Discovery uncovered that the immediate initiative was eSIMs, but the larger problem was UNA's ability to launch multiple third-party services without turning every category into a separate product and engineering project.
>
> For the Head of Partnerships, the problem was securing the right merchant or service supply and moving those partnerships toward launch.
>
> For Product, it was creating a coherent in-app experience that could extend beyond one category without losing control of the customer journey.
>
> For Engineering, it was determining whether the integration was feasible, how authentication and payment-related handoffs would work, who would maintain the layer and whether using Boxo was more sensible than hiring additional engineers and building internally.
>
> For the CEO, the initiative connected to a broader strategic objective: expanding what customers could do inside the UNA app, launching new commercial categories more quickly and creating additional monetization opportunities.
>
> The measurable ambition eventually became approximately 100 merchant launches within the first year. That transformed the conversation from “Can we integrate an eSIM provider?” into “How does UNA build a repeatable merchant-launch capability?”

**Discovery structure:**

- **Current initiative:** Launch eSIMs.
- **Underlying constraint:** Each new category risked becoming bespoke engineering work.
- **Desired capability:** Repeatable onboarding of additional services and merchants.
- **Executive outcome:** Faster platform expansion and additional monetization.
- **Measurable target:** Approximately 100 merchant launches in the first year.

**Raw-source basis:** The stakeholder needs and reusable-infrastructure thesis come from C1 and C2. The eSIM-first sequence and 100-merchant target are Seth-confirmed corrections from 2026-07-21.

### 121. Boxo/UNA: Why was it qualified, and what would have disqualified it?

> I would describe the qualification using MEDDICC because this was a complex enterprise opportunity rather than a single-stakeholder product sale.
>
> The metric was a contractual target of approximately 100 merchant launches within the first year, supported by a broader goal of adding new service categories faster.
>
> The economic buyer was the CEO, who ultimately approved the decision.
>
> The decision criteria included launch speed, integration feasibility, customer experience, support ownership, vendor maturity, data and responsibility boundaries, and whether Boxo created a better path than hiring engineers and building internally.
>
> The decision process moved from the Head of Partnerships into Product and Engineering validation, through a lightweight RFP and legal/compliance review, and finally to CEO approval.
>
> The identified pain was that repeatedly building new merchant and service categories internally would consume engineering capacity, slow the roadmap and create ongoing maintenance work.
>
> My champion was the Head of Partnerships, who owned the immediate launch initiative and helped us navigate the wider organization.
>
> The main competition was internal build and the status quo, not another vendor or a pricing discount.
>
> I would have disqualified or slowed the opportunity if the eSIM initiative were merely exploratory, if the Head of Partnerships could not widen access, if Product or Engineering rejected the architecture, if the risk process revealed an unsupported requirement or if UNA wanted extensive custom work without a credible commercial commitment.

**MEDDICC-style qualification:**

- **Metrics:** Approximately 100 merchant launches in one year; faster category expansion.
- **Economic buyer:** CEO.
- **Decision criteria:** Feasibility, experience, support, risk, vendor maturity and build-versus-buy economics.
- **Decision process:** Partnerships → Product/Engineering → lightweight RFP and risk review → CEO.
- **Identify pain:** Engineering capacity, roadmap delay and repeated maintenance.
- **Champion:** Head of Partnerships.
- **Competition:** Internal build and status quo.
- **Paper process:** Lightweight RFP, legal/compliance review and commercial agreement.

**Raw-source basis:** MEDDICC is being used as an interview explanation of the verified deal evidence. It is not claimed as Boxo's official historical methodology.

### 122. Boxo/UNA: Who was the champion, economic buyer and wider buying group?

> The Head of Partnerships was my champion because they owned the initial eSIM initiative, benefited directly from a workable launch path and helped move the conversation into the other teams required for a decision.
>
> The CEO was the economic buyer and final decision-maker. The CEO cared about expanding the platform, accelerating merchant launches and creating additional engagement and monetization without waiting for every initiative to be built internally.
>
> The Product Head evaluated whether the services would fit UNA's roadmap and deliver a coherent in-app experience.
>
> Engineering evaluated the SDK and integration model, authentication and payment-related handoffs, maintenance requirements, support ownership and the build-versus-buy comparison.
>
> Legal, compliance and risk evaluated data boundaries, third-party responsibility, service levels, liability and whether UNA could rely on a young vendor inside a regulated customer experience.
>
> Internally, I coordinated Boxo's Product and Engineering teams. My job was to translate the buyer's concerns into precise questions, secure supportable answers and return without overstating what the platform could do.
>
> Multi-threading was not about bypassing the Head of Partnerships. It was about helping the champion make the initiative defensible to every stakeholder who could approve, implement or stop it.

**Buying-group map:**

| Stakeholder | Role in the decision | Principal concern |
|---|---|---|
| Head of Partnerships | Champion | Partner supply and launch progress |
| CEO | Economic buyer and final decision-maker | Expansion, speed, monetization and strategic risk |
| Product Head | Product evaluator | Roadmap, experience and reusable launch model |
| Engineering | Technical evaluator | Feasibility, maintenance, ownership and internal-build comparison |
| Legal/compliance/risk | Risk evaluators | Data, liability, service levels and vendor maturity |
| Boxo Product and Engineering | Internal solution team | Supportable scope, technical answers and delivery capacity |

**Raw-source basis:** The Head of Partnerships champion and CEO decision authority are Seth-confirmed. C1 and C2 supply the broader Product, Engineering and risk map.

### 123. Boxo/UNA: What proof and objections controlled the deal?

> The principal objection was not price. It was whether UNA should use Boxo or hire additional engineers and build the capability internally.
>
> I respected that internal build was a legitimate option. UNA had technical talent, so telling Engineering that it could not build the platform would have damaged credibility.
>
> I reframed the comparison around the complete operating burden: how quickly UNA wanted to launch, how many merchants and categories it expected to support, how much engineering capacity would be required, who would maintain every integration and what other roadmap work would be delayed.
>
> Vendor maturity was the related risk. Boxo was still a young company, so the buyer needed confidence around integration feasibility, support ownership, data boundaries, service levels and what would happen if a merchant workflow failed.
>
> We created proof through the process itself. I brought the right Boxo Product and Engineering specialists into the evaluation, translated the buyer's questions precisely, completed the lightweight RFP and stayed honest about what was supported versus what required additional work.
>
> We won by making the decision defensible: relevant supplier insight, a clear first use case, a feasible technical path, explicit ownership and a stronger expansion model than repeatedly hiring and building category by category.

**How we handled build versus buy:**

1. Accepted that internal build was technically possible.
2. Compared total engineering and maintenance requirements rather than feature parity.
3. Connected the decision to launch speed and roadmap opportunity cost.
4. Made implementation and support ownership explicit.
5. Used the RFP and technical process to establish credibility.
6. Anchored the first use case to a repeatable merchant-expansion model.

> [!warning] Truth boundary
> No material pricing objection or unverified discount will be introduced into the story.

### 124. Boxo/UNA: How did you build the stakeholder-specific business case?

> I built one business case around faster, repeatable service expansion, then translated it into the language of each stakeholder.
>
> For the Head of Partnerships, the value was gaining a credible way to source and launch service partners, beginning with eSIMs and later expanding into additional merchants.
>
> For Product, the value was adding new categories through a coherent embedded experience rather than sending customers through disconnected external journeys.
>
> For Engineering, the case was avoiding the need to hire and maintain enough internal capacity to build every merchant integration separately. The value was not that Boxo could eliminate engineering; it was that UNA could reserve its engineers for differentiated roadmap work.
>
> For legal, compliance and risk, the case depended on explicit ownership: what data moved where, who supported each workflow, what service expectations applied and how third-party risk would be managed.
>
> For the CEO, those threads came together as a platform-growth decision. UNA could launch more services and monetization opportunities faster without making every new category dependent on internal hiring and bespoke development.
>
> The approximately 100-merchant contractual target gave the case a measurable operational outcome. Each stakeholder could therefore defend the same decision through the result they personally owned.

**One value spine, different stakeholder lenses:**

| Stakeholder | Business case |
|---|---|
| Partnerships | Source and launch more merchant partners |
| Product | Create a coherent, reusable in-app service experience |
| Engineering | Reduce repeated build and maintenance burden |
| Legal/compliance/risk | Establish explicit control and responsibility boundaries |
| CEO | Accelerate platform expansion and monetization |
| Shared metric | Approximately 100 merchant launches within one year |

**Raw-source basis:** The stakeholder responsibilities come from C1 and C2. The unified value spine is a source-faithful interview synthesis rather than a historical Boxo artifact.

### 125. Boxo/UNA: How did you manage the commercial process and close?

> I managed the opportunity over approximately six months because technical validation, stakeholder alignment and enterprise review all had to converge before the CEO could approve the decision.
>
> I owned the day-to-day commercial process: maintaining the account thesis, coordinating stakeholders, shaping scope and pricing, translating questions into Boxo's Product and Engineering teams and keeping each next step tied to an unresolved decision.
>
> UNA ran a lightweight RFP. I coordinated the response across the relevant internal teams and made sure the commercial promise remained aligned with what Boxo could actually support.
>
> There was no material pricing objection. The central economic comparison was the cost and operating burden of using Boxo versus hiring additional engineers, building the infrastructure internally and maintaining every merchant integration over time.
>
> Legal, compliance and risk then worked through the responsibility and contractual boundaries. The CEO was the final decision-maker.
>
> My closing discipline was to avoid treating the proposal or RFP response as proof that the deal had advanced. Progress meant that a real blocker had been resolved, the appropriate stakeholder had validated the answer and the next decision was explicit.
>
> After approximately six months, we reached agreement on an initial eSIM scope worth approximately USD 200K ARR.

**Commercial progression:**

1. Confirmed the initial eSIM scope and wider expansion thesis.
2. Validated Product and Engineering feasibility.
3. Completed the lightweight RFP.
4. Compared Boxo with internal engineering investment.
5. Resolved legal, compliance, risk and responsibility questions.
6. Secured CEO approval.
7. Closed at approximately USD 200K in initial ARR.

> [!warning] Truth boundary
> Do not invent a discount, concession history or detailed contract-redline chronology.

### 126. Boxo/UNA: How did onboarding and expansion work?

> I treated onboarding as part of the commercial promise because the value depended on merchant launches, not simply on signing the agreement.
>
> We began with eSIMs as the initial use case. That allowed UNA and Boxo to establish the working model: how partners would be onboarded, how the embedded experience would operate, what Product and Engineering owned and how support and risk issues would be handled.
>
> Once that model was working, the relationship expanded into e-commerce and a broader set of merchant partners. As more merchants were onboarded, the commercial relationship expanded from approximately USD 200K to approximately USD 300K ARR.
>
> The agreement targeted approximately 100 merchant launches within the first year, and UNA achieved that target.
>
> That is the most important expansion proof. This was not simply additional contracted scope. The customer used the model to launch the merchant volume it had committed to, demonstrating that the initial platform decision could become a repeatable operating capability.
>
> My land-and-expand lesson was to begin with a strategically connected wedge, make the implementation model repeatable and let successful adoption support the next category and commercial expansion.

**Expansion progression:**

1. **Land:** Initial eSIM use case at approximately USD 200K ARR.
2. **Operationalize:** Establish the partner-onboarding and implementation model.
3. **Expand:** Add e-commerce and additional merchant partners.
4. **Deliver:** Achieve the contractual target of approximately 100 merchant launches in one year.
5. **Commercialize:** Grow the relationship to approximately USD 300K ARR.

**Source correction:** Seth confirmed on 2026-07-21 that eSIMs were the initial use case and e-commerce was the subsequent expansion. This supersedes older C2 language that reversed the sequence.

### 127. Boxo/UNA: What did you personally own, and what did you learn?

> I personally owned the commercial motion from account selection through expansion.

>

>

>
> The initial agreement was approximately USD 200K ARR. It expanded to approximately USD 300K, and UNA achieved the contractual target of approximately 100 merchant launches within the first year.

A champion helps me navigate the organization. Each stakeholder then needs different proof before the decision becomes safe enough to approve and implement.
>
> That experience also became part of the Boxo playbook I could reuse with accounts such as Epon: begin with a specific expansion initiative, align the commercial and technical stakeholders, make build versus buy concrete and turn the first use case into a broader platform relationship.

**What I personally owned:**

| Stage | Seth's ownership |
|---|---|
| Account selection | Identified UNA as a priority account |

| Outbound | Contacted the Head of Partnerships through LinkedIn |

| Multi-threading | Developed the champion and coordinated the buying group |
| Technical orchestration | Translated buyer concerns into precise internal questions |
| Commercial process | Managed pricing, the lightweight RFP and negotiation |
| Close | Progressed the account to CEO approval and signature |
| Expansion | Stayed involved as the scope moved into e-commerce and more merchants |

**Shorter version:**

### 127A. Boxo/UNA: What went wrong, and how did you recover?

> [!tip] FINAL-ROUND EDIT 2026-07-26: NEW FOLLOW-UP
> This adds the missing failure-and-recovery layer to the existing UNA bank. Use the verified six-month cycle and approximately USD 200K-to-USD 300K commercial values, not the unreconciled live-transcript figures.

**75–90 second answer:**

> What went wrong at UNA was that I had a strong commercial champion, but I let the deal stay in that lane for too long.
>
> The Head of Partnerships was excited and circulated our material, but Product and Engineering were deciding whether to build internally. They were receiving second-hand explanations, and my champion did not have the technical authority to carry that case alone. I had mistaken champion enthusiasm for buying-group alignment.
>
> I recovered by arranging an in-person meeting in the Philippines with Boxo's CEO, UNA's engineering lead, and the champion. That moved us from forwarded sales material into direct business and technical evaluation. We could address build versus buy, implementation, and support ownership together.
>
> I then multi-threaded the wider group, coordinated Boxo's Product and Engineering teams, completed the lightweight RFP, and worked through legal and risk. The process took approximately six months. UNA signed at approximately USD 200K in initial ARR, later expanded to approximately USD 300K, and achieved its target of approximately 100 merchant launches in year one.
>
> The lesson was to test a champion by whether they can help create direct access to the people who must approve, implement, or stop the decision. In a technical enterprise deal, the seller owns buying-group alignment early.

**Drill-down version:**

> What went wrong was that I had a strong commercial champion and good early momentum, but I allowed the deal to stay in that lane for too long.
>
> The Head of Partnerships was excited and circulated the material internally, but Product and Engineering were evaluating a build-versus-buy decision. They were receiving second-hand explanations rather than questioning Boxo directly, and my champion did not have the technical authority to carry that case alone. I had mistaken champion enthusiasm for buying-group alignment.
>
> I recovered by naming the missing stakeholders and creating a reason for direct alignment. I arranged an in-person meeting in the Philippines with Boxo's CEO, UNA's engineering lead, and the champion. That changed the conversation from forwarded sales material into a real business and technical evaluation. We could address the internal-build option directly, surface implementation and support concerns, and agree what Product, Engineering, and Boxo each needed to validate.
>
> From there, I multi-threaded the wider group, coordinated Boxo's Product and Engineering teams, completed the lightweight RFP, worked through legal and risk, and kept the commercial decision tied to a repeatable merchant-launch capability rather than one integration.
>
> The process took approximately six months. UNA signed at approximately USD 200K in initial ARR, later expanded to approximately USD 300K, and achieved its contractual target of approximately 100 merchant launches in the first year.
>
> The lesson was not simply "travel more." It was to test a champion by whether they can help create direct access to the people who must approve, implement, or stop the decision. In a technical enterprise deal, the seller owns buying-group alignment early; a champion should not be asked to translate the whole case alone.

**What failed:** champion-only momentum without direct Product and Engineering alignment.

**Recovery:** direct multi-threading → in-person buyer alignment → technical evaluation → RFP and risk process → CEO decision.

**Result:** approximately USD 200K initial ARR → approximately USD 300K after expansion; approximately 100 merchants launched in year one.

> [!warning] Truth boundary
> The qualitative miss and Philippines recovery meeting are supported by Seth's July 22 account. The controlling commercial facts are the later verified approximately six-month cycle, USD 200K initial ARR, USD 300K expanded ARR, and approximately 100 merchant launches. Do not use the transcript's unreconciled eight-month, USD 250K, or USD 350K figures.

## XII. Symmetrical Flagship Drill: Airwallex And EU Holidays

### 128. Airwallex/EU Holidays: What was your role, scoreboard and result?

> I joined Airwallex as the second outbound hire for Singapore. I was a full-cycle, self-sourcing AE without SDR support, so I owned the complete new-business motion: account selection and tiering, research, prospecting, discovery, qualification, stakeholder mapping, solution positioning, commercial negotiation, signature, compliance, activation and initial expansion.
>
> My quota was individual and measured in gross profit. The annual target was approximately USD 500,000 in GP. It was not a revenue, ARR, contract-value or transaction-volume quota.
>
> Underneath that annual number, I had three operating measures.
>
> First, I had a formal floor of ten new accounts live each month. Live meant that the account had passed compliance and been created. I planned toward eleven activations—two Tier 1, three Tier 2 and six Tier 3—to create some protection against compliance or onboarding slippage.
>
> Second, those accounts needed to demonstrate early commercial quality. The accounts currently inside their first 30 days needed to produce approximately USD 5,000 in aggregate GP. The accounts inside their first 60 days needed to produce approximately USD 7,500 in aggregate GP. That prevented me from meeting the activation number with accounts that went live but never meaningfully transacted.
>
> Third, I managed the mix of the book. Tier 1 and Tier 2 were the primary proactive-prospecting lanes, while Tier 3 came through more scaled and opportunistic channels such as events, inbound interest, referrals and broader campaigns.
>
> Across the year, I activated approximately 120 accounts: 22 Tier 1, approximately 30 Tier 2 and approximately 68 Tier 3. The Tier 1 accounts generally matured into approximately USD 40,000 to USD 60,000 in annual GP, although EU Holidays became an outlier at approximately USD 100,000 after expansion.
>
> In total, I generated approximately USD 1 million in actual GP against the approximately USD 500,000 individual annual quota. That put me at approximately 200% attainment.
>
> The important distinction is that the tier thresholds represented estimated mature annual GP used for prioritization, while quota attainment was based on GP actually generated during the measured year. Because accounts activated at different points throughout the year, I would not multiply every tier threshold by every logo and present that as recognized GP.
>
> What Airwallex proved for me was that I could build my own pipeline, manage a high-volume operating cadence and still create larger strategic accounts through disciplined, full-cycle execution.

**Airwallex scoreboard:**

| Metric | Target or result |
|---|---:|
| **Role** | Full-cycle, self-sourcing AE |
| **Singapore-team position** | Second outbound hire |
| **SDR support** | None |
| **Quota owner** | Individual |
| **Quota basis** | Gross profit |
| **Annual quota** | Approximately USD 500K GP |
| **Annual GP produced** | Approximately USD 1M |
| **Quota attainment** | Approximately 200% |
| **Monthly activation floor** | 10 accounts |
| **Monthly operating target** | 11 accounts |
| **Monthly tier mix** | 2 Tier 1, 3 Tier 2 and 6 Tier 3 |
| **Annual activations** | Approximately 120 |
| **Annual Tier 1 result** | 22 accounts |
| **Annual Tier 2 result** | Approximately 30 accounts |
| **Annual Tier 3 result** | Approximately 68 accounts |
| **F30 quality gate** | Approximately USD 5K aggregate GP |
| **F60 quality gate** | Approximately USD 7.5K aggregate GP |
| **Typical Tier 1 economics** | Approximately USD 40K–60K in mature annual GP |
| **EU Holidays result** | Approximately USD 100K in mature annual GP after expansion |

**What each metric meant:**

| Metric | Definition |
|---|---|
| **Activation** | Compliance passed and the customer account was created |
| **F30 GP** | Aggregate GP from accounts currently within their first 30 days |
| **F60 GP** | Aggregate GP from accounts currently within their first 60 days |
| **Tier estimate** | Provisional mature annual GP used to prioritize and allocate seller effort |
| **Actual GP** | Gross profit generated during the measured quota year |
| **Attainment** | Actual annual GP divided by the individual annual GP quota |

**Monthly operating mix:**

| Tier | Expected mature annual GP | Primary coverage | Monthly activation target | Approximate annual result |
|---|---:|---|---:|---:|
| **Tier 1** | More than USD 30K | Bespoke one-to-one pursuit | **2** | **22** |
| **Tier 2** | USD 10K–30K | One-to-few campaigns | **3** | **30** |
| **Tier 3** | Below USD 10K | Scaled or opportunistic coverage | **6** | **68** |
| **Total** | — | Blended book | **11** | **Approximately 120** |

**How the scoreboard worked together:**

1. **Create sufficient new coverage:** Maintain a floor of ten accounts live per month.
2. **Plan above the floor:** Work toward eleven to absorb compliance or activation slippage.
3. **Protect the account mix:** Generate two Tier 1, three Tier 2 and six Tier 3 activations.
4. **Test early monetization:** Produce approximately USD 5K across the F30 cohort and USD 7.5K across the F60 cohort.
5. **Build the annual book:** Activate approximately 120 accounts across the three tiers.
6. **Deliver the commercial outcome:** Generate approximately USD 1M in actual GP against the approximately USD 500K individual quota.

**Shorter version:**

> I was the second outbound hire for Singapore and a full-cycle, self-sourcing AE without SDR support. I owned account selection and prospecting through discovery, negotiation, activation and expansion.
>
> My individual annual quota was approximately USD 500,000 in gross profit. Underneath that, I had a floor of ten accounts live each month and operated toward eleven: two Tier 1, three Tier 2 and six Tier 3.
>
> The book also had early-quality gates. Accounts inside their first 30 days needed to generate approximately USD 5,000 in aggregate GP, and accounts inside their first 60 days needed to generate approximately USD 7,500.
>
> Across the year, I activated approximately 120 accounts—22 Tier 1, 30 Tier 2 and 68 Tier 3—and generated approximately USD 1 million in GP. That put me at approximately 200% of my individual quota.

**If asked for the result in one sentence:**

> I generated approximately USD 1 million in annual GP against an individual quota of approximately USD 500,000, or about 200% attainment, while activating approximately 120 accounts across the year.

**Memorable summary:**

> Activation built the book. F30 and F60 tested its early quality. Actual GP determined quota attainment.

**Strongest source material:**

- C1 establishes Seth's role as Airwallex's second outbound hire in Singapore, his full-cycle ownership, GP quota, activation system, tier mix and approximately USD 1M result.
- C2 supports the distinction between outbound activity, account activation, early monetization and mature account value.
- The recruiter-screen strategy establishes that Airwallex should prove conventional quota-carrying and repeatable full-cycle execution.
- The hiring-leader debrief requires one metric per sentence, explicit metric bases and separation of verified results from reconstructed operating estimates.
- S2 supports explaining quota performance through a repeatable pipeline-generation and territory-management cadence rather than raw activity alone.

**Raw-source basis:** C1 records a Seth-confirmed correction on 2026-07-21 that the approximately USD 500K figure was the full-productivity individual annual GP quota, superseding the earlier ramp-adjusted reconstruction. C1 also confirms approximately USD 1M in actual GP, approximately 200% attainment, the monthly ten-account activation floor, the planned 2/3/6 tier mix and approximately 120 annual activations across 22 Tier 1, 30 Tier 2 and 68 Tier 3 accounts. The F30 and F60 aggregate-GP gates were confirmed by Seth on 2026-07-20. EU Holidays' approximately USD 100K mature annual GP result supersedes the earlier USD 60K figure.

> [!warning] Truth boundary
> Never say that Seth exceeded a USD 1M quota by 200%. The individual annual quota was approximately USD 500K GP, and approximately USD 1M was the actual GP produced. Do not describe GP as revenue, ARR, ACV, contract value or TPV. Do not multiply the tier thresholds by every activated account and present the result as recognized GP. The annual tier counts are approximate, and the GP bands used before discovery were provisional estimates rather than customer-confirmed economics.

### 129. Airwallex/EU Holidays: How did you define the territory, ICP and anti-ICP?

> My Singapore territory broadly covered companies below the enterprise cutoff—approximately SGD 50 million in annual revenue and 500 employees. That was the upper boundary of my coverage, not the minimum qualification threshold, so my territory contained Tier 1, Tier 2 and Tier 3 accounts.
>
> I did not work every account inside that territory equally. I focused primarily on travel and retail because both gave me observable signals of international payment requirements.
>
> Within travel, I divided the market into outbound tour operators, inbound agencies, destination-management companies and corporate-travel agencies. Outbound tour operators were generally the strongest fit because they regularly paid overseas hotels, guides, ground operators and other suppliers across several currencies and under tight deadlines. I could identify some of that before a meeting by reviewing the tours and destinations they advertised.
>
> Many inbound, destination-management and corporate-travel businesses were weaker fits for that particular motion because they often needed credit-facility functionality that Airwallex did not provide at the time. They were not bad businesses; their primary workflow simply did not align as closely with the product we could sell successfully.
>
> Within retail, I started with store footprint. More than ten stores—particularly in central or high-footfall locations—was normally a strong signal of operating scale. I then reviewed the retailer's ecommerce website to identify where its products were manufactured. Overseas manufacturing suggested potential supplier payments and gave me an initial hypothesis about the relevant countries, currencies and payment corridors.
>
> Those were research signals rather than confirmed economics. A retailer might manufacture overseas but buy through a local distributor, so discovery still needed to confirm whom it actually paid and how the payment workflow operated.
>
> Before meeting the customer, I could only estimate its likely GP band. Tier 1 represented more than USD 30,000 in expected annual GP, Tier 2 represented USD 10,000 to USD 30,000 and Tier 3 represented less than USD 10,000.
>
> We were strongly encouraged to concentrate proactive prospecting on Tier 1 and Tier 2. Tier 1 received bespoke one-to-one account planning, while Tier 2 received targeted one-to-few campaigns. Tier 3 still belonged in my territory, but I generally engaged those accounts through conferences, trade shows, inbound interest, referrals, broader campaigns or a compelling new signal rather than investing heavily in manual outbound.
>
> Because that GP band was provisional before discovery, my practical prioritization can also be summarized as a ten-point Account Priority Score. I assessed commercial scale out of three points, international payment complexity out of two, product breadth out of two, reason to act now out of two, and access and proof out of one.
>
> Accounts scoring eight to ten justified immediate, deeply researched attention. Accounts scoring five to seven entered an active targeted campaign or close monitoring. Accounts below five generally remained in scaled coverage or nurture until stronger evidence appeared.
>
> Discovery was where I replaced those assumptions with customer facts. I would confirm actual payment volume, frequency, currencies, corridors, current providers and product requirements, then revise the GP projection and tier if necessary.
>
> EU Holidays scored highly before the first formal meeting. It was a scaled outbound operator; its European and China tours implied meaningful international payment complexity; it had credible multi-product potential; its brochures gave me a specific reason to reach out; and the travel fair, partner evidence and my experience with a smaller competitor gave me a credible access and proof path.
>
> That justified Tier 1-style preparation even though I had not yet confirmed its internal payment volumes. Discovery subsequently validated the opportunity, and the account ultimately generated approximately USD 100,000 in mature annual GP after expansion.

**Account Priority Score:**

| Criterion | Points | Pre-meeting evidence |
|---|---:|---|
| **Commercial scale** | 0–3 | Company footprint, number of stores, breadth of tour operations or other observable indicators of meaningful payment volume |
| **International payment complexity** | 0–2 | Overseas suppliers, multiple currencies, difficult corridors, recurring international payments or time-sensitive execution |
| **Product breadth** | 0–2 | Potential for one narrow product versus a credible path across several Airwallex products |
| **Reason to act now** | 0–2 | Generic fit, an account-specific hypothesis or a visible initiative, deadline, expansion or operational trigger |
| **Access and proof** | 0–1 | An identifiable buyer, event interaction, warm path, partner evidence or relevant customer proof |
| **Total** | **0–10** | Determines immediate account priority |

**Score-to-action bands:**

| Score | Pre-meeting treatment |
|---:|---|
| **8–10** | Immediate focus and deeper manual preparation |
| **5–7** | Active targeted campaign or close monitoring |
| **0–4** | Scaled coverage or nurture until stronger evidence appears |

**EU Holidays illustrative pre-meeting score:**

| Criterion                            |    Score | Evidence available before discovery                                                                       |
| ------------------------------------ | -------: | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Commercial scale**                 |      3/3 | Established outbound operator with a meaningful tour footprint                                            |
| **International payment complexity** |      2/2 | European and China tours implied recurring payments across several corridors                              |
| **Product breadth**                  |      2/2 | Credible path beyond the initial FX and transfer wedge                                                    |
| **Reason to act now**                |      1/2 | Strong account-specific hypothesis, but internal urgency was not yet confirmed                            |
| **Access and proof**                 |      1/1 | Travel-fair interaction, identifiable Head of Finance, partner evidence and smaller-competitor experience |
| **Total**                            | **9/10** | Immediate priority and deep manual preparation                                                            |

**Shorter version:**

> My territory covered Singapore companies below an approximate enterprise cutoff of SGD 50 million in revenue and 500 employees. Within that territory, I focused on travel and retail.
>
> For travel, outbound tour operators were the strongest fit because they regularly paid overseas hotels, guides and suppliers across several currencies. For retail, I looked for more than ten stores and used the ecommerce website to identify where products were manufactured. Store footprint indicated scale, while overseas manufacturing suggested potential international supplier payments.
>
> Before discovery, the GP tier was provisional: Tier 1 was above USD 30,000, Tier 2 was USD 10,000 to USD 30,000 and Tier 3 was below USD 10,000. We were encouraged to focus proactive prospecting on Tier 1 and Tier 2.
>
> I then used a ten-point Account Priority Score covering commercial scale, international payment complexity, product breadth, reason to act now and access or proof. EU Holidays scored highly because it was a scaled outbound operator with visible European and China corridors, multi-product potential, a specific message and a credible access path.
>
> Discovery then replaced those assumptions with actual customer facts, and the account ultimately expanded to approximately USD 100,000 in mature annual GP.

**Memorable summary:**

> Territory determined what I owned. Vertical fit showed where we could win. The provisional GP band estimated potential. The Account Priority Score determined what I worked first. Discovery replaced assumptions with customer facts.

**Raw-source basis:** C1 establishes Airwallex's Singapore coverage, Seth's focus on travel and retail and the reconciled EU Holidays account. C2 supports the travel-market segmentation, outbound-tour-operator ICP, credit-facility anti-ICP and workflow-based territory method. Seth clarified on 2026-07-21 that the approximately SGD 50M revenue and 500-employee figures represented the upper boundary of his territory, which included all three GP bands, while proactive prospecting was concentrated on Tier 1 and Tier 2. Seth also confirmed that more than ten retail stores, particularly in central locations, was a strong scale signal and that manufacturing origin was generally available through the retailer's ecommerce website. The GP bands and scorecard criteria were reconciled with Seth on the same date.

> [!warning] Truth boundary
> Before discovery, the GP band was provisional rather than customer-confirmed. Store count, store location, destination mix and manufacturing origin were research signals, not proof of payment volume. Do not claim that overseas manufacturing proved the retailer paid a foreign manufacturer directly. Describe the ten-point Account Priority Score as a concise reconstruction of the prioritization logic unless the exact numeric score was formally recorded at Airwallex.

### 130. Airwallex/EU Holidays: Why did this account deserve priority?

> EU Holidays received Tier 1-style attention because it scored approximately nine out of ten on my pre-meeting Account Priority Score.
>
> The GP band was still provisional because I had not yet confirmed its internal payment volumes. However, the observable evidence was strong enough to justify deeper manual investment.
>
> It scored three out of three for commercial scale. It was an established outbound tour operator with a meaningful tour footprint and sufficient visible activity to suggest material payment requirements.
>
> It scored two out of two for international payment complexity. Its brochures showed that it specialized in European and China tours. That implied recurring overseas payments across several currencies and corridors, including China, where payment execution could be more difficult.
>
> It scored two out of two for product breadth. I could see a credible path beyond a single FX or transfer use case into several Airwallex products over time.
>
> It scored one out of two for reason to act now. The destination mix gave me a strong account-specific hypothesis, but I had not yet confirmed internal urgency. I treated that as something to validate through discovery rather than pretending my research had already proven the problem.
>
> It scored one out of one for access and proof. I had spoken with one or two EU Holidays representatives at a travel fair, identified the Head of Finance as the intended entry point, assembled relevant partner proof and had already started working with a smaller competitor in the same market.
>
> The score explained why the account deserved attention, but the preparation showed what Tier 1 coverage meant in practice.
>
> I first watched several Chorus recordings from the UK team to understand why travel was an important Airwallex vertical. At the travel fair, I collected EU Holidays' brochures, spoke with its representatives and identified its emphasis on European and China tours.
>
> I then developed an account-specific commercial hypothesis. Airwallex had historically competitive pricing across the relevant European payment rails, while China could be a more difficult corridor. My hypothesis was that EU Holidays might be experiencing more than high FX costs: payment infrastructure could also be affecting supplier-payment reliability, reconciliation and the destinations its Commercial team could confidently support.
>
> Before approaching the office, I mapped the likely buying committee. I expected Finance to care about FX, payment execution and reconciliation; Operations to care about suppliers receiving funds reliably during live tours; Commercial to care about expanding destinations; the CFO to care about financial risk; and the CEO to make the final decision.
>
> I chose the Head of Finance as my entry point and prepared a specific opening tied to EU Holidays' European and China tour operations. I also created a slide deck specifically for the account and built a preliminary ROI-calculator spreadsheet.
>
> The calculator was hypothesis-based. Its purpose was to structure the value conversation and allow me to replace assumptions with the customer's actual payment volumes, costs, failure rates and reconciliation workload during discovery. I would not present the initial calculations as confirmed customer economics.
>
> My initial email and LinkedIn outreach did not generate engagement. Because the account scored highly and justified bespoke attention, I changed the channel rather than placing it into an endless generic sequence. I went directly to its office in the People's Park travel cluster and asked specifically for the Head of Finance.
>
> I was therefore not door-knocking blindly. By the time I entered the office, I had an account-specific hypothesis, a preliminary buying-committee map, a deliberate entry persona, relevant proof, a tailored presentation and a preliminary ROI model.
>
> Discovery subsequently confirmed that the problem was real and broader than FX. The account eventually generated approximately USD 100,000 in mature annual GP after expanding across FX, card spend and the payment gateway.
>
> That is why EU Holidays deserved priority: the pre-meeting evidence justified the investment, the bespoke preparation created a credible path into the account and discovery validated the commercial potential.

**EU Holidays pre-meeting score:**

| Criterion | Score | Evidence available before discovery |
|---|---:|---|
| **Commercial scale** | 3/3 | Established outbound operator with a meaningful tour footprint |
| **International payment complexity** | 2/2 | European and China tours implied recurring payments across several currencies and corridors |
| **Product breadth** | 2/2 | Credible path beyond the initial FX and transfer wedge |
| **Reason to act now** | 1/2 | Strong account-specific hypothesis, but internal urgency had not been confirmed |
| **Access and proof** | 1/1 | Travel-fair interaction, identifiable Head of Finance, partner evidence and smaller-competitor experience |
| **Total** | **9/10** | Immediate priority and deep manual preparation |

**Why the timing score was not 2/2:**

> The research showed relevance and gave me a reason to investigate. It did not prove that EU Holidays had an active initiative, executive deadline or urgent internal problem.
>
> That distinction matters because a strong hypothesis earns outreach; confirmed urgency has to come from the buyer.

**What Tier 1 preparation meant:**

| Dimension                 | EU Holidays preparation                                                                   |
| ------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Vertical research**     | Watched UK-team Chorus calls to understand the travel use case                            |
| **Account research**      | Collected brochures and studied the advertised European and China tours                   |
| **Customer interaction**  | Spoke with one or two representatives at the travel fair                                  |
| **Commercial hypothesis** | Connected destination mix to FX, payment reliability, reconciliation and commercial reach |
| **Buyer map**             | Mapped Finance, Operations, Commercial, CFO and CEO                                       |
| **Entry strategy**        | Selected and explicitly requested the Head of Finance                                     |
| **Opening**               | Prepared around EU Holidays' actual tour destinations and likely payment corridors        |
| **Proof**                 | Prepared relevant partner evidence and smaller-competitor experience                      |
| **Collateral**            | Created a presentation specifically for EU Holidays                                       |

### 131. Airwallex/EU Holidays: What research produced your point of view?

> I first developed the vertical thesis by watching a few Chorus call recordings from Airwallex's UK team. Travel was already a core vertical there, and those conversations gave me an early understanding of why the sector fit Airwallex: recurring cross-border payments, destination-specific corridors, seasonal volume and a high consequence when payments failed. I used that as a starting hypothesis for Singapore rather than assuming the market would behave identically.
>
> The account-specific insight came from attending a travel fair and picking up EU Holidays' brochures. When I reviewed the products they were actively selling, I saw that they specialized heavily in European and China tours.
>
> That destination mix gave me two concrete angles. For their European tours, I knew Airwallex had very competitive pricing across the relevant European payment rails. For China, the issue was less about price and more about execution: it was a difficult corridor where other providers often encountered compliance delays or outright payment problems.
>
> My point of view was therefore specific: EU Holidays could potentially improve its European-tour economics while making its China payment workflow more reliable. I also hypothesized that those payment problems could affect live-tour execution, reconciliation and the Commercial team's confidence in adding new destinations.
>
> I took that into the conversation as a hypothesis rather than pretending I already knew their internal numbers. Discovery then confirmed and quantified the problem: payment reliability needed to move from approximately 80% to greater than 99%, most outright rejections involved China and month-end reconciliation consumed approximately ten person-days.
>
> The lesson for me was that the UK Chorus calls created the vertical thesis, but the travel-fair brochure created the account point of view. One told me where to look; the other told me what might matter specifically to EU Holidays.

**Research chain:**

1. **Learn the vertical:** UK Chorus calls showed why travel could be a successful Airwallex vertical.
2. **Collect account evidence:** Picked up EU Holidays' brochures at a travel fair.
3. **Identify the destination mix:** Europe and China were major tour categories.
4. **Map the commercial angles:** Europe → competitive payment-rail pricing; China → compliance, processing and reliability risk.
5. **Form the hypothesis:** Improve European-tour economics and China payment execution.
6. **Validate in discovery:** Confirm the reliability, reconciliation and growth gaps with the buyer.

**Shorter version:**

> I initially learned why travel could be a strong vertical by watching a few Chorus calls from the UK team, where travel was already a core part of the business.
>
> For EU Holidays specifically, I attended a travel fair, picked up their brochures and saw that they specialized in European and China tours. That gave me two account-specific angles: Airwallex had highly competitive pricing across European payment rails, while China was a difficult corridor where other providers often encountered compliance and processing problems.
>
> My point of view was that EU Holidays could improve its European-tour economics and make its China payment workflow more reliable. I took that in as a hypothesis, and discovery later confirmed the reliability and reconciliation problems.

**Raw-source basis:** C1 establishes the destination-to-corridor research, Airwallex coverage comparison and connection to operational and commercial consequences. Seth confirmed on 2026-07-21 that the vertical thesis began with a few UK-team Chorus recordings where travel was a core vertical; the EU Holidays account insight came from brochures collected at a travel fair; the brochures showed a specialization in European and China tours; Airwallex had strong historical pricing on the relevant European payment rails; and China was known as a problematic corridor. C2 supports the broader travel-fair, destination-mix and travel-workflow method. Describe the rail and pricing advantages as historical Airwallex positioning at the time. 2. **Develop the insight:** Map advertised destinations to payment corridors and provider weaknesses.
3. **Test conventional channels:** Email and LinkedIn did not generate engagement.
4. **Change the access path:** Use direct office outreach for a high-priority account.
5. **Earn the conversation:** Discuss EU Holidays' actual workflow and operating risk.

### 133. Airwallex/EU Holidays: How did you move a priority account forward week by week?

> I did not treat "book a meeting" as the only goal for a strategic account. A Tier 1 account could remain in focus for several weeks, but each week needed a specific advancement objective.
>
> I would begin by asking three questions: What stage is the account at? What evidence is missing? What is the smallest meaningful objective I can achieve this week?
>
> Before engagement, the objectives were usually about improving the account thesis or access path. That could mean confirming the operating model, identifying the current provider, mapping the likely buying committee, finding relevant customer proof or determining which stakeholder owned the workflow.
>
> Once the buyer engaged, the objectives changed. I needed to validate the problem, quantify the impact, identify the champion, widen the stakeholder map, understand the decision process and agree on the proof required to move forward.
>
> Every interaction updated the plan. If I learned that my original hypothesis was wrong, I changed the message or deprioritized the account. If I confirmed the problem but lacked access, the next objective became stakeholder expansion. If the buyer believed the value but lacked confidence, the next objective became proof or risk reduction.
>
> EU Holidays is a good example. I did not jump directly from identifying the company to repeatedly asking for a meeting.
>
> My first objective was to validate whether the travel vertical and EU Holidays' operating model justified deeper attention. I watched UK-team Chorus calls, attended the travel fair, collected its brochures and spoke with one or two representatives. That established the European and China destination mix.
>
> My next objective was to turn that information into an account-specific thesis. I connected its destinations to the likely payment corridors and developed a hypothesis around FX, payment reliability and reconciliation.
>
> I then mapped the likely buying committee and selected the Head of Finance as the initial entry point. I assembled relevant partner and competitor proof, prepared a tailored presentation and built a preliminary ROI calculator.
>
> Email and LinkedIn did not produce engagement, so the account had not advanced. Instead of repeating the same activity, I changed the access strategy. I went to the office and asked specifically for the Head of Finance.
>
> Once I earned the conversation, the objective moved from access to validation. Discovery confirmed the payment-reliability and reconciliation problems and gave me the customer facts required to refine the commercial case.
>
> From there, each stage had a different evidence goal: develop the Head of Finance as a champion, engage Operations and Commercial, align the CFO and CEO, agree on a controlled FX starting point, complete the commercial process, pass compliance, activate usage and then expand.
>
> The operating principle was that an account only advanced when I learned something material, gained relevant access, secured buyer agreement or removed a decision risk. Activity alone did not count as progress.

**Account Advancement Plan:**

> Current stage → missing evidence → weekly objective → actions → exit criterion → updated account thesis

**EU Holidays advancement plan:**

This is a stage-based reconstruction, not a verified calendar of exact historical weeks.

| Stage | Weekly objective | Actions | Evidence of advancement |
|---|---|---|---|
| **1. Validate fit** | Determine whether EU Holidays' operating model justified deeper research | Review travel-sector calls, collect brochures and speak with representatives | European and China tours confirmed as relevant account evidence |
| **2. Build the thesis** | Identify a specific commercial problem worth testing | Map destinations to payment corridors and Airwallex positioning | Account-specific hypothesis covering FX, reliability and reconciliation |
| **3. Map access** | Identify the initial buyer and wider committee | Map Finance, Operations, Commercial, CFO and CEO | Head of Finance selected as the entry point |
| **4. Prepare proof** | Make the first conversation credible | Assemble partner proof, smaller-competitor context, tailored deck and preliminary ROI model | Relevant proof and customer-specific assets ready |
| **5. Test the entry path** | Determine whether digital outreach could create engagement | Email and LinkedIn outreach | No engagement; evidence that the channel needed to change |
| **6. Change the channel** | Create direct access to the workflow owner | Visit the office and request the Head of Finance | Finance conversation secured |
| **7. Validate the problem** | Replace hypotheses with customer facts | Run discovery across payments, reliability and reconciliation | Approximately 80% reliability, delays, rejections and ten person-days of reconciliation uncovered |
| **8. Qualify the opportunity** | Confirm value, stakeholders, process and proof requirements | Apply MEDDICC and define the initial use case | Real opportunity established rather than general interest |
| **9. Multi-thread** | Build support beyond the champion | Engage Operations, Commercial, CFO and CEO | Business, operational and risk cases aligned |
| **10. Reduce risk** | Give the buyer a safe first commitment | Agree on controlled FX volume and relevant proof | Buyer accepts a limited initial scope |
| **11. Commercialize** | Convert the validated case into an agreement | Own proposal, negotiation and signature | Agreement signed in approximately two months |
| **12. Activate and expand** | Turn signature into usage and broader GP | Support compliance, activation and adjacent products | Approximately USD 100K in mature annual GP after expansion |

**Weekly account-review template:**

| Field | Question |
|---|---|
| **Current stage** | Where is the account based on buyer evidence? |
| **Account thesis** | What do I currently believe about the workflow and value? |
| **Missing evidence** | What remains unknown or unproven? |
| **Weekly objective** | What specific learning, access, agreement or proof will advance the account? |
| **Actions** | Which stakeholders, channels and assets will I use? |
| **Exit criterion** | What observable evidence would count as progress? |
| **Result** | What happened this week? |
| **Updated thesis** | What changed based on the evidence? |
| **Next objective** | What is now the most important gap? |
| **Disposition** | Advance, continue, recycle, nurture or disqualify? |

**Shorter version:**

> I did not use "book a meeting" as the only goal for strategic accounts. I kept priority accounts in focus across multiple weeks, but each week needed a specific advancement objective tied to the missing evidence.
>
> For EU Holidays, the progression was: validate the travel fit, identify its destination mix, build the corridor hypothesis, map the buying committee, select the Head of Finance, prepare relevant proof and then test the access path.
>
> When email and LinkedIn did not work, I did not count the activity as progress. I changed the channel and approached the office directly.
>
> Once I earned the conversation, the objectives changed from access to validation, qualification, stakeholder expansion, proof, commercial agreement and activation.
>
> An account advanced when I learned something material, gained relevant access, secured buyer agreement or removed a decision risk—not merely because I completed another touch.

**Memorable summary:**

> Every week needed one missing piece of evidence, one account objective and one observable exit criterion.

**Strongest source material:**

- [Salesforce Sales Account Plans](https://help.salesforce.com/s/articleView?id=sf.account_plans.htm&language=en_US) and its [living-account-plan guidance](https://www.salesforce.com/blog/sales-rep-guide-to-better-planning/) support measurable objectives, action plans, milestones and ongoing plan revision.
- [Gong and Accord's deal-execution guidance](https://www.gong.io/blog/accord-gong-bridging-customer-insights-and-deal-execution) supports turning discovery into a buyer-specific action plan reflecting priorities, timeline, stakeholders and required assets.
- S1 and S4 support advancing accounts through buyer evidence, stakeholder access, missing proof and decision-risk removal rather than seller activity alone.

**Raw-source basis:** The EU Holidays research, outreach, Head of Finance entry point, stakeholder map, discovery metrics, controlled FX wedge, approximately two-month sales cycle and approximately USD 100K mature annual GP result are taken from the Seth-confirmed, reconciled account in C1. The stage sequence is a structured reconstruction of those confirmed events, informed by C2, S1, S2 and S4; it is not a CRM-exported weekly history.
