Describe it as the account-advancement logic visible in the confirmed EU Holidays motion. Do not claim that activity alone advanced the account; progression required new evidence, stakeholder access, buyer agreement or risk removal.

### 134. Airwallex/EU Holidays: What did discovery uncover?

> I entered the conversation with a hypothesis about FX and payment corridors, but discovery showed that the problem was much broader. I uncovered three measurable gaps: payment reliability, reconciliation and commercial reach.
>
> First, payment reliability needed to move from approximately 80% to greater than 99%. Roughly 20% of payments were experiencing bank-side compliance delays, and approximately 2% were rejected outright, mostly on payments to China. The consequence was operational: guides or suppliers could be waiting for funds while a tour was already live.
>
> Second, month-end reconciliation required five people working for roughly two full days—about ten person-days every month. Errors were common, particularly double-counted transactions, so the team had to spend time tracing the records to find and correct them. Their desired state was a mostly automated process where Finance reviewed exceptions and performed a quick final check.
>
> Third, the payment infrastructure was affecting commercial growth. They wanted stronger corridor coverage that could support new products, including African safari tours.
>
> What made the opportunity important was the connection between those gaps. Payment performance affected live operations, reconciliation consumed Finance capacity and created control risk, and corridor limitations constrained new revenue opportunities. Discovery therefore moved the conversation from “Can you offer better FX?” to “Can we build a more reliable and scalable payments operation?”

**Current state → target state:**

- **Payment reliability:** Approximately 80% → greater than 99%.
- **Compliance-related delays:** Approximately 20% → exception rather than routine.
- **Outright rejections:** Approximately 2%, mostly China payments → minimized.
- **Reconciliation effort:** Five people × two days → mostly automated with a quick Finance review.
- **Reconciliation quality:** Common double-counting and manual investigation → clear exception review.
- **Commercial reach:** Insufficient support for planned African safari tours → coverage capable of supporting those destinations.

**Shorter version:**

> Discovery moved the conversation well beyond FX pricing.
>
> Payment reliability needed to improve from roughly 80% to above 99%. Around 20% of payments experienced compliance-related delays, and about 2% were rejected outright, mostly for China. That could affect guides and suppliers while tours were already live.
>
> Month-end reconciliation also took five people two full days, with common double-counting errors that required manual investigation. They wanted that process mostly automated with Finance performing a quick final review.
>
> Finally, stronger corridor coverage could support new commercial products, including African safari tours. So the opportunity connected payment reliability, Finance capacity, operational continuity and commercial growth.

**Raw-source basis:** C1 establishes that discovery moved beyond FX price into payment reliability, reconciliation and control, live-tour execution and corridor-limited growth. Seth confirmed on 2026-07-21 the current-state and target-state measures: approximately 80% payment reliability toward a greater-than-99% target; approximately 20% compliance-related delays; approximately 2% outright rejections, mostly for China payments; five people working for two days at month-end; common double-counting errors requiring investigation; a desired mostly automated reconciliation process; and desired support for African safari tours. C2 supports the broader travel-vertical workflow and stakeholder context. QMD retrieval from S1 supports the current-reality → measurable-gap → cause → consequence discovery method; that methodology does not establish Seth's career facts.

> [!warning] Metric and outcome boundaries
> Treat the approximately 20% delayed and approximately 2% rejected figures separately because it is not yet established whether they overlap. These are current-state and desired-state discovery measures, not confirmed post-implementation results. Africa was a desired commercial opportunity; do not claim the safari-tour expansion was ultimately launched without separate confirmation. No exact reconciliation error rate has been verified.

### 135. Airwallex/EU Holidays: Why was it qualified, and what would have disqualified it?

> I qualified EU Holidays using a MEDDICC framework.
>
> - **Metrics:** Payment reliability needed to move from approximately 80% to greater than 99%. Approximately 20% of payments experienced bank-side compliance delays, while approximately 2% were rejected outright, mostly on payments to China. Month-end reconciliation needed to move from five people working for two full days—approximately ten person-days per month—to a mostly automated process with a quick Finance review. Errors were common, particularly double-counted transactions that required manual investigation. Commercially, they wanted to move from insufficient corridor support for planned African safari tours to payment coverage capable of supporting those destinations.
> - **Economic buyer:** The CEO was the final decision-maker. The CFO remained important to the financial-control and risk case, but the CEO made the ultimate decision.
> - **Decision criteria:** The solution needed to improve payment reliability toward the greater-than-99% target, reduce compliance-related delays and outright rejections, simplify reconciliation, maintain appropriate financial controls, provide responsive support and support corridors connected to future tour growth.
> - **Decision process:** The Head of Finance owned the workflow and acted as my champion. Operations validated the consequences of delays during live tours, Commercial validated the value of unlocking additional destinations, the CFO evaluated the financial-control implications and the CEO made the final decision. We then used FX and controlled volume as the initial proof step.
> - **Identify pain:** The pain was much larger than FX pricing. Payment delays could affect guides and suppliers while tours were already operating. Payments to China faced particular rejection risk. Reconciliation consumed approximately ten person-days every month and included investigating double-counted transactions, while corridor limitations constrained products such as African safari tours.
> - **Champion:** The Head of Finance was a genuine champion because they owned the problem, helped me navigate the buying group and supported a controlled path to action. The test was their behavior and ability to mobilize the process, not whether they liked the product.
> - **Competition:** The primary competition was the existing provider and trusted-bank status quo. Relevant partner proof helped significantly, particularly because I had already started working with a smaller competitor of EU Holidays. That reduced the fear of being first. The controlled FX rollout then allowed EU Holidays to validate Airwallex inside its own workflow.
>
> That is why I considered it qualified. We had measurable gaps, an accountable champion, a mapped buying group, a confirmed final decision-maker, clear decision criteria and a controlled proof path. I would have slowed or disqualified it if any of those foundations—especially meaningful volume, recurring pain, champion strength, executive access or a safe proof workload—were missing.

**Current state → target state:**

- **Payment reliability:** Approximately 80% → greater than 99%.
- **Compliance-related delays:** Approximately 20% of payments delayed → delays becoming exceptions rather than routine.
- **Outright rejections:** Approximately 2%, mostly involving China payments → rejection risk minimized.
- **Month-end effort:** Five people for two full days, or approximately ten person-days per month → mostly automated reconciliation with a quick Finance review.
- **Reconciliation quality:** Common errors and manual investigation of double-counted transactions → exceptions identified clearly enough for Finance to review and approve.
- **Commercial reach:** Insufficient corridor support for planned African safari tours → payment coverage capable of supporting those destinations.

**Shorter version:**

> I qualified EU Holidays using MEDDICC. The metrics were concrete: payment reliability needed to move from roughly 80% to above 99%, and month-end reconciliation needed to move from five people working for two days to a mostly automated process with a quick Finance review. The existing manual process also produced common errors, particularly double-counted transactions that Finance had to trace and correct. Commercially, they wanted better corridor coverage to support new products such as African safari tours.
>
> The Head of Finance was my champion, Operations and Commercial validated the wider impact, the CFO evaluated financial-control risk and the CEO was the final decision-maker. The decision criteria were reliability, reconciliation, control, support and corridor coverage. The incumbent provider and trusted-bank status quo were the competition, and we used relevant partner proof plus controlled FX volume to create a safe evaluation path.
>
> I would have slowed the opportunity down if there were no measurable pain, credible champion, executive decision path or meaningful workload through which we could prove value.

**Raw-source basis:** C1 establishes the recurring cross-border pain, Head of Finance champion, Operations and Commercial stakeholders, controlled FX proof path and historical disqualification conditions. Seth confirmed on 2026-07-21 that the CEO was the final decision-maker; this supersedes the earlier ambiguity that grouped the CFO and CEO together as final decision-makers. Seth also confirmed the current-state and target-state measures: approximately 80% payment reliability toward a greater-than-99% target; approximately 20% compliance-related delays; approximately 2% outright rejections, mostly for China payments; five people working for two days at month-end; common double-counting errors requiring investigation; a desired mostly automated reconciliation process; and desired support for African safari tours. C2 supports the travel-vertical workflow, stakeholder and switching-risk context. QMD retrieval from S1, S2 and S3 supports milestone-based qualification and explicit walk-away conditions; those sources do not establish Seth's career facts.

> [!warning] Metric and outcome boundaries
> Treat the approximately 20% delayed and approximately 2% rejected figures separately because it is not yet established whether they overlap. The current-state → target-state measures are buyer goals, not confirmed post-implementation results. Africa was a desired commercial opportunity; do not claim Airwallex ultimately unlocked African safari tours without separate confirmation. No exact reconciliation error rate has been verified.

### 136. Airwallex/EU Holidays: Who was the champion, economic buyer and wider buying group?

> The Head of Finance was my champion. They were not simply the person who responded to me; they owned the payment workflow and the outcomes we were trying to improve—cost, reliability, reconciliation and control.
>
> I knew they were a real champion because they helped me understand the wider decision, supported a controlled path to action and gave us a route into the other stakeholders who needed to evaluate the change. I look for behavior and access, not enthusiasm, when deciding whether someone is genuinely a champion.
>
> I worked through that relationship rather than going around it. We involved the Head of Operations because Operations could validate the consequences of payment failure during live tours. We involved the Head of Commercial because Commercial could connect stronger corridor coverage to the ability to serve more destination markets.
>
> The CFO was important to the financial-control and risk case, while the CEO was the final decision-maker. That distinction matters: the CFO helped evaluate whether moving volume was financially and operationally safe, but the CEO held the ultimate decision authority.
>
> My multi-threading principle is to map each stakeholder to a decision question. Finance owned the workflow, Operations validated execution risk, Commercial validated growth value and the executive layer decided whether the overall risk and return justified moving forward.

**Stakeholder map:**

1. **Champion — Head of Finance:** Owned the payment problem, helped navigate the account and supported the proof path.
2. **Operational validator — Head of Operations:** Validated live-tour execution risk and the consequences of payment failure.
3. **Commercial validator — Head of Commercial:** Connected corridor coverage to additional destinations and commercial opportunity.
4. **Financial-risk stakeholder — CFO:** Evaluated the financial-control and business-continuity implications.
5. **Economic buyer and final decision-maker — CEO:** Held the ultimate decision authority.

**Shorter version:**

> The Head of Finance was my champion because they owned payment cost, reliability, reconciliation and control. I knew they were a real champion because they helped navigate the internal process and gave us access to the stakeholders required for a decision.
>
> Through that relationship, I involved Operations to validate live-tour execution risk and Commercial to validate the value of broader corridor coverage. The CFO evaluated the financial-control risk, and the CEO was the final decision-maker.
>
> What I can say confidently is that the Head of Finance mobilized the process, the CFO evaluated the financial-risk case and the CEO made the final decision.

### 137. Airwallex/EU Holidays: What proof and objections controlled the deal?

> The hardest objection was not price. It was whether EU Holidays should trust a fintech with payment volume that had historically moved through established banks.
>
> From the buyer's perspective, that was completely rational. If a payment failed during a live tour or Finance lost control of the workflow, the operational consequences could outweigh the potential savings. I did not try to talk them out of that concern with a broad claim that Airwallex was better.
>
> Instead, I made the objection testable. We agreed to use FX as the initial wedge and begin with controlled volume rather than asking them to migrate everything. The proof question was simple: could Airwallex execute an important payment workflow reliably and provide the level of support the customer needed?
>
> Relevant partner proof also helped significantly. I had already started working with a smaller competitor of EU Holidays, so I could show that Airwallex was being validated by a company operating in the same market, with similar corridors and seasonal payment pressure. I did not share anything confidential, but that nearby proof made the decision feel less experimental. They were not being asked to become the first travel company to trust the model.
>
> Once they saw the product and support perform in their own workflow, the Head of Finance had evidence that the relationship could be expanded safely. That was stronger than a presentation or a generic customer logo because the evidence came from their own operation.
>
> I believe we won because we combined an account-specific corridor point of view, relevant proof from nearby travel partners and a low-risk way to validate Airwallex in EU Holidays' own workflow. My approach to objections is to acknowledge the underlying risk, define what evidence would resolve it and design the smallest meaningful proof that can produce that evidence.

**How the objection was resolved:**

1. **Acknowledge the risk:** Moving volume from trusted banks created legitimate operational and control concerns.
2. **Define the proof question:** Could Airwallex execute a relevant workflow reliably and support the customer when needed?
3. **Bring proximate proof:** Used relevant partner experience—including work with a smaller competitor—to reduce the fear of being first.
4. **Reduce the commitment:** Began with FX and controlled volume instead of requiring a full migration.
5. **Use customer evidence:** Let performance inside EU Holidays' workflow support the case for broader adoption.

**Shorter version:**

> The hardest objection was trust. EU Holidays had moved payment volume through established banks for years, so the concern was whether changing providers could create operational or control risk.
>
> I did not try to overcome that with a broad platform pitch. Relevant proof from travel partners helped, especially because I had already started working with a smaller competitor of theirs. Then we started with FX and controlled volume, which allowed EU Holidays to test Airwallex in a meaningful workflow and evaluate product and support reliability for itself.
>
> Once that worked, the Head of Finance had evidence from their own operation to support expansion. My objection-handling principle is to bring nearby proof, make the remaining concern testable and let controlled execution answer it.

**Raw-source basis:** The trusted-bank objection, controlled FX wedge, product-and-support reliability proof and subsequent evidence-led expansion are taken from the user-confirmed, reconciled EU Holidays account in C1. Seth additionally confirmed on 2026-07-21 that partner proof contributed significantly and that he had already started working with a smaller EU Holidays competitor; the competitor remains unnamed and no confidential performance information is claimed. C2 supports the broader travel-vertical trust, switching-risk, proof-proximity and controlled-rollout method, but its detailed legitimacy questions, implementation controls and support practices are not all attributed specifically to EU Holidays. QMD retrieval from S1, S2 and S3 supports the methodology that proof should match the buyer's workflow and risk model and provide the safest credible path to a larger commitment; those sources do not establish Seth's career facts.

### 138. Airwallex/EU Holidays: How did you build the stakeholder-specific business case?

> I built the business case around one shared workflow problem, then translated its consequences into the language of each stakeholder. I did not want to create four disconnected pitches.
>
> For Finance, the case was FX economics, transfer reliability, reconciliation and control. They owned the payment workflow, so they needed confidence that Airwallex could improve the economics without weakening oversight.
>
> For Operations, the issue was execution. An urgent overseas payment failing while a tour was already live could disrupt guides, suppliers and the customer experience. Reliability and responsive support therefore mattered as much as price.
>
> For Commercial, the value was broader corridor coverage. If Finance could support more destination markets reliably, the company had greater flexibility to launch and operate additional tours.
>
> For the CFO and CEO, I brought those threads together. The executive case was not simply that Airwallex offered cheaper FX. It was that the platform could support commercial growth and operating resilience without introducing unacceptable control or business-continuity risk.
>
> The Head of Finance helped carry that case internally. My job was to make sure every stakeholder could explain the same decision through the outcome they personally owned. That is how I think about multi-threaded business cases: one value spine, different stakeholder evidence.

**How the business case was built:**

1. **Find the shared workflow:** Cross-border payments supporting outbound tours.
2. **Map the consequences:** Economics and control, operational continuity, destination expansion and executive risk.
3. **Translate by stakeholder:** Explain the same opportunity using the outcome each person owns.
4. **Unify the decision:** Give the champion an executive-level story connecting growth, resilience and controlled adoption.

**Shorter version:**

> I built one business case and translated it across the buying group.
>
> Finance cared about FX economics, reliability, reconciliation and control. Operations cared about overseas payments succeeding during live tours. Commercial cared about supporting more destination markets. The CFO and CEO needed confidence that those benefits could be achieved without creating unacceptable control or business-continuity risk.
>
> The Head of Finance helped carry that case internally. My role was to make sure every stakeholder could defend the same decision in the language of the outcome they owned.

**Raw-source basis:** The stakeholder map and four value lenses are taken from the user-confirmed, reconciled EU Holidays account in C1. C2 supports the broader travel-vertical method of translating one payment workflow across Finance, Operations, Commercial and executive stakeholders, but its specific transfer-fee calculations, reconciliation-time estimates and ROI package are not attributed to EU Holidays. QMD retrieval from S1 and S3 supports the methodology that a champion needs a concise, forwardable internal story and that buyer use of the business case matters more than seller-produced collateral; those sources do not establish Seth's career facts.

### 139. Airwallex/EU Holidays: How did you manage the commercial process and close?

> I managed the close by reducing the size of the decision without reducing the value of the opportunity.
>
> EU Holidays already had established banking relationships, so I did not try to force a full-platform migration. We agreed that the first commercial scope would be FX with controlled volume. That gave them a meaningful workflow to validate while limiting the operational and control risk of changing providers.
>
> From there, I kept each next step tied to a concrete decision. We worked through expected volume, what implementation would require, who would own the rollout and what the executive decision-makers needed to approve. That helped me distinguish real deal progress from meetings that simply felt positive.
>
> I owned the process from discovery and positioning through commercial negotiation and signature. During the commercial discussion, my focus was keeping the initial scope large enough to prove real value but controlled enough that the buyer could defend the decision internally.
>
> We signed the agreement approximately two months after first contact. My closing principle was that I should not manufacture urgency or push for the largest possible first commitment. I should create a clear, low-risk path through which the customer can make a confident decision—and then earn the larger relationship through execution.

**How the close progressed:**

1. **Control the scope:** Positioned FX and controlled volume as the defensible first commitment.
2. **Resolve concrete decisions:** Worked through volume, implementation, rollout ownership and executive approval.
3. **Maintain ownership:** Carried the opportunity from discovery and positioning through negotiation and signature.
4. **Close on confidence:** Signed approximately two months after first contact without requiring an immediate full-platform migration.

**Shorter version:**

> I managed the close by reducing the size of the initial decision without reducing its commercial value. EU Holidays already trusted its banks, so instead of forcing a full migration, we agreed to begin with FX and controlled volume.
>
> I tied each next step to a real decision—expected volume, implementation requirements, rollout ownership or executive approval—and owned the process through negotiation and signature. We signed approximately two months after first contact.
>
> My principle was to make the first commitment safe enough to approve but meaningful enough to prove value. That created the foundation for the later expansion.

**Raw-source basis:** Full-cycle ownership, controlled FX scope, the four decision categories and the approximately two-month sales cycle are taken from the user-confirmed, reconciled EU Holidays account in C1. C2 establishes that Seth's broader Airwallex playbook covered commercial negotiation, closing questions, next steps and stakeholder escalation, but does not establish the exact EU Holidays chronology. QMD retrieval from S1 and S2 supports the methodology that deals should advance through buyer validation and concrete milestones rather than seller activity or optimism; those sources do not establish Seth's career facts. The exact discount, pricing concessions, proposal sequence, procurement stages and contract redlines remain unknown and are intentionally excluded. A formal mutual action plan is not claimed.

### 140. Airwallex/EU Holidays: How did onboarding and expansion work?

> I treated onboarding as part of the commercial process because, at Airwallex, signature was not the finish line. An account only created value once it was live, using the product and producing gross profit. Our F30 and F60 measurements reinforced that discipline.
>
> With EU Holidays, we started with FX and controlled volume rather than asking them to move their entire financial workflow away from established banks. I stayed involved through onboarding so we could get that first use case operational, resolve friction quickly and prove that both the product and our support were reliable.
>
> Once the initial workflow was working, the expansion conversation became evidence-based. We could look at adjacent problems inside the same finance operation and ask where else the platform could improve control, reconciliation, payments or employee spending. The relationship expanded into transfers, multi-currency accounts, cards, expense management and reconciliation, online payment acceptance and offline point-of-sale.
>
> After bundling FX, card spend and the payment gateway, the account reached approximately USD 100K in mature annual gross profit.
>
> The lesson I took from that was that land-and-expand should follow realized value. Activate a narrow but meaningful wedge, make the success visible and then expand into adjacent workflows using the trust you have already earned.

**How onboarding and expansion progressed:**

1. **Activate the wedge:** Began with FX and controlled volume rather than forcing an immediate full-platform migration.
2. **Prove reliability:** Stayed involved through onboarding so the initial workflow became operational and the customer could validate product and support performance.
3. **Expand adjacently:** Used proven usage to expand into related finance, spending and payment workflows.
4. **Quantify the outcome:** The bundle of FX, card spend and payment gateway reached approximately USD 100K in mature annual GP.

**Shorter version:**

> I treated onboarding as part of the sale because signature did not generate GP—activated usage did. With EU Holidays, we landed through FX and controlled volume, stayed close through onboarding and proved product and support reliability before asking them to expand.
>
> Once that first workflow worked, we expanded into adjacent needs including transfers, multi-currency accounts, cards, expense management and reconciliation, online payments and offline POS. After bundling FX, card spend and the payment gateway, the account reached approximately USD 100K in mature annual GP.
>
> My land-and-expand principle is simple: activate the wedge, prove the value and let evidence earn the broader relationship.

**Raw-source basis:** The controlled FX wedge, continued onboarding involvement and listed expansion products are taken from the user-confirmed, reconciled EU Holidays account in C1. Seth corrected the expanded result to approximately USD 100K in mature annual GP on 2026-07-21, superseding the earlier USD 60K figure. C1 also establishes Airwallex's activation, F30 and F60 measurement model. C2 supports the broader hands-on travel-agency onboarding and land-and-expand method, but its detailed one-hour setup, access-control configuration and WhatsApp support workflow are not attributed specifically to EU Holidays. QMD retrieval from S1 and S3 supports the methodology that adoption creates internal pull and that a low-risk implementation path builds buyer confidence; those sources do not establish Seth's career facts. Exact adoption dates, transaction volumes and implementation milestones remain unknown and are intentionally excluded.

### 141. Airwallex/EU Holidays: What did you personally own, and what did you learn?

> I owned the entire commercial motion, from account selection through expansion. I selected EU Holidays from the travel vertical, researched the destinations they were selling and mapped the relevant payment corridors. When email and LinkedIn did not work, I changed the channel, went to their office and reached the Head of Finance.
>
> I ran discovery, developed the Head of Finance as my champion and multi-threaded into Operations, Commercial, the CFO and the CEO. We landed with FX and controlled volume. I carried the deal through negotiation and signature, stayed involved during onboarding and expanded it across adjacent finance and payment products. It signed in approximately two months and reached approximately USD 100K in mature annual gross profit.
>
> The repeatable lesson was that in a trust-heavy sale, I should start with a narrow, painful workflow, bring an account-specific point of view, adapt the channel when necessary and prove reliability in a controlled scope. Once the first use case works, expansion is earned rather than merely pitched.

**How the ownership progressed:**

1. **Territory and account selection:** Chose outbound travel as a priority vertical and selected EU Holidays using cross-border workflow fit.
2. **Research and access:** Built a destination-to-corridor thesis, then changed from digital outreach to direct office outreach when the original channels failed.
3. **Opportunity development:** Ran discovery, developed the Head of Finance as the champion and multi-threaded the operational, commercial and executive buying group.
4. **Commercial control:** Landed with FX and controlled volume, carried the process through negotiation and signature, and avoided forcing a high-risk full-platform migration.
5. **Realization and expansion:** Stayed involved through onboarding, proved reliability and expanded into adjacent finance and payment workflows.

**Simple example:**

> The buyer already trusted its banks, so asking them to move everything at once would have increased resistance. I started with controlled FX volume, gave them a way to validate Airwallex in a real workflow and used the resulting trust to expand the relationship.

**Raw-source basis:** The career facts are taken from the user-confirmed, reconciled EU Holidays account in C1: direct office outreach after email and LinkedIn failed; Head of Finance champion; Operations and Commercial stakeholders; full-cycle ownership through onboarding and expansion; and approximately two months to signature. Seth corrected the expanded result to approximately USD 100K in mature annual GP on 2026-07-21 and also confirmed that the CFO evaluated the financial-risk case and the CEO was the final decision-maker. C2 supplies the deeper vertical-playbook and low-risk-wedge framing. QMD retrieval from S1, S2 and S3 supports the methodology that seller judgment should become a repeatable framework and that buyer confidence is built through relevant research, controlled proof and clear ownership. Those methodology sources do not establish Seth's career facts. The exact discount, proposal sequence and procurement chronology remain unknown and are intentionally excluded.

## Questions To Ask The Account Director

Choose two after hearing what the interviewer emphasizes:

1. What distinguishes the Strategic BDRs whom Account Directors trust most with their accounts?
2. What evidence makes an opportunity genuinely qualified rather than merely an interested meeting?
3. Where do you want the BDR to retain ownership after you join the opportunity?
4. What are the most common ways a BDR creates noise or weakens an Account Director's strategy?
5. If I earned your trust in the first six months, what would I be doing consistently?

## Drafting Order

The core sales-acumen evidence spine is drafted across Section II and Questions 24–141, with Questions 106 and 108 intentionally held for personal-story confirmation. Remaining work should proceed in this order:

1. Confirm the personal events required for Questions 106 and 108.
2. Draft Questions 6–12 using the now-stable commercial evidence spine; Questions 1–5 are already complete.
3. Run a final deduplication, spoken-length and truth-boundary pass across the complete pack.

## Truth-Boundary Backlog

- [ ] UNA exact LinkedIn touch sequence; do not invent it.
- [ ] UNA detailed RFP, legal-review and contract-redline chronology; do not invent it.
- [ ] UNA company revenue; do not use the unsupported USD 500M estimate.
- [ ] Airwallex historical quarterly ramp allocation; do not present the illustrative pattern as fact.
- [ ] Airwallex run-rate pipeline and close-rate denominator.
- [ ] EU Holidays exact discount, proposal and procurement chronology.
- [ ] Question 106: confirm a real hard-feedback event, including who delivered it, the setting, the exact feedback and the observed behavior change. Do not present the CRM-hygiene candidate as fact yet.
- [ ] Question 108: confirm a real cross-functional disagreement, including the competing positions, decision and verified outcome.

## Completion Standard

An entry is complete only when it has:

- a 30–60-second speakable answer;
- a direct first sentence;
- one primary example;
- one verified metric or concrete buyer action where relevant;
- explicit personal ownership;
- a raw-source basis;
- no unsupported precision;
- no duplicated answer that belongs under a different question;
- a short follow-up module only when the interviewer is likely to probe.
